React Native & Expo: Build and Ship a Mobile App · Participant Workbook

Part 1: Foundations and Lists

Sessions 1 to 9. Modules 1 to 3: how React Native turns components into native views and what Expo adds, a working toolchain (Node LTS, VS Code with Expo Tools, the shoreline-api mock back end, an Expo SDK 58 project running in Expo Go or the web preview), the shared code that comes over from Shoreline Crew HQ, then the core components and layout (View, Text and StyleSheet, flexbox, images, icons, fonts and safe areas, touch and input, responsive, platform-specific and themed UI) and fast lists (FlatList, SectionList, FlashList). Runs on its own as a short course and ends with the Part 1 project check-in and assessment.

Instructor Kit · Participant Workbook

Session 1: How React Native Works and Your Toolchain

Your workbook for Session 1: the environment check, a diagnostic, the key ideas of Lessons 1.1 and 1.2, worked examples, the guided labs, predict-then-check practice, a self-check for each lesson, and your exit ticket.

Lessons 1.1–1.2 Session 1 · 3 hours Name: ____________________

Session Goals

  • Find out whether your computer is ready: the environment check ends with READY, and you know what to do if it does not.
  • Explain what React Native does with your components (native views, no DOM) and what Expo adds.
  • Say what carries over from React on the web and what changes on a phone.
  • Run your toolchain: Node LTS, VS Code, the course API on :4000 and Metro on :8081, each in its own terminal, and the app in Expo Go or the web preview.
  • Tell running apart from checking: Metro (and Jest) run your code; npx tsc --noEmit and npx expo lint check it.

Start Here: Is Your Computer Ready?

Environment check
  • ☐ Node.js LTS (24 or newer) and VS Code with Expo Tools are installed; node --version in a NEW terminal starts with v24 or more
  • ☐ My course folder shoreline holds shoreline-api (unzipped, server.ts directly inside), shoreline-crew (created with npx create-expo-app@latest) and the course's labs/ folder
  • ☐ Expo Go is on my phone (or I will use the web preview)
  • ☐ Terminal 1: cd shoreline-api, then node server.ts (left running)
  • ☐ Terminal 2, in shoreline: node labs/module-01/l1_0-orientation/check_environment.mjs
  • ☐ The last line says READY for React Native & Expo: Build and Ship a Mobile App (or READY WITH NOTES)
  • ☐ If it said NOT READY, I wrote my plan here (from the install clinic) and will finish it before Session 2:

Five messages to know by heart: node is not recognized = open a new terminal (or install Node) · ERR_UNKNOWN_FILE_EXTENSION = your Node is too old · nothing answers at http://localhost:4000 = start the API · no shoreline-crew here = wrong folder, or create the project · the phone cannot connect = same Wi-Fi? Local Network? WSL? else the web preview.

Diagnostic: predict, then check

Six questions on the React and TypeScript this course builds on: filter and map, copying with one change, ??, narrowing, state and the terminal. Circle your answer before the class reveals it. Nobody collects this.

Missed one? The skill and where it comes back: D1 filter and map (every list from Lesson 1.3 on (Module 3's FlatList renders exactly this)) · D2 copying with one change (every state update (Lesson 2.4 on) and TanStack Query's cache (Module 5)) · D3 || and ?? (Lesson 1.3 (the HQ's helpers) and every value that can legitimately be 0) · D4 TypeScript narrowing (Lesson 1.3 (type guards) and every status, role and screen state) · D5 React state (Lesson 2.4 (touch and input) and every press handler) · D6 folders and the terminal (Lesson 1.2 (two terminals, the right folder) and every npx command after it). For practice at home: labs/module-01/l1_0-orientation/diagnostic_starter.mjs has JavaScript answer D1, D2 and D3 (in new examples), one small mistake each.

D1
What does this print?
const events = [
  { title: 'Weekend Cove Cleanup', capacity: 18, spotsTaken: 18 },
  { title: 'Jetty Rocks Deep Clean', capacity: 10, spotsTaken: 9 },
]
console.log(events.filter((e) => e.spotsTaken < e.capacity).map((e) => e.title))
A) [ 'Weekend Cove Cleanup' ]
B) [ 'Jetty Rocks Deep Clean' ]
C) [ 9 ]
D) [ 'Weekend Cove Cleanup', 'Jetty Rocks Deep Clean' ]
D2
What does this print?
const jetty = { title: 'Jetty Rocks Deep Clean', capacity: 10, spotsTaken: 9 }
const signedUp = { ...jetty, spotsTaken: jetty.spotsTaken + 1 }
console.log(jetty.spotsTaken, signedUp.spotsTaken)
A) 10 10
B) 9 9
C) 9 10
D) An error: jetty is a const
D3
What does this print?
const spotsLeft = 0
console.log(spotsLeft || 'unknown', spotsLeft ?? 'unknown')
A) 0 0
B) unknown unknown
C) 0 unknown
D) unknown 0
D4
What type does TypeScript give status on the last line?
type Status = 'scheduled' | 'completed' | 'cancelled'
function label(status: Status) {
  if (status === 'cancelled') return 'Cancelled'
  return status
}
A) Status
B) string
C) 'scheduled' | 'completed'
D) never
D5
count starts at 0. One press runs setCount(count + 1) twice. What does the screen show after the press?
A) 2 signed up
B) 1 signed up
C) 0 signed up
D) An error: state set twice
D6
Your terminal is in your home folder; the course folder is shoreline inside it. Which runs the environment check?
A) node check_environment.mjs
B) cd shoreline, then node labs/module-01/l1_0-orientation/check_environment.mjs
C) npm run check_environment
D) npx expo check_environment

Lessons

1.1

How React Native Works

By the end you can
  • Explain how React Native turns your components into native views, and why there is no DOM on a phone
  • Describe the New Architecture in plain words: Hermes, JSI, the Fabric renderer and native modules
  • Sort what carries over from React on the web and what changes on a phone
  • Say what Expo adds (the CLI, the SDK, Expo Router, Expo Go, development builds, EAS) and when each one is used
  • Describe the Shoreline Crew phone app and how it shares an API and types with Shoreline Crew HQ
Key ideas
  • No DOM on a phone: React Native's components stand for the phone's own native views. <View> is the box (div), <Text> holds all text (no loose text), <Pressable> with onPress makes something tappable, StyleSheet.create replaces CSS (camelCase, numbers without units, no selectors or cascade).
  • The web preview is a translation: react-native-web turns the same components into HTML (a View becomes a <div>). Use it for layout and data; confirm native behavior on a phone.
  • The New Architecture: Hermes runs your JavaScript inside the app; JSI lets it call native code directly; Fabric renders native views; native modules (TurboModules) give it the camera, location, secure storage. "The bridge" is the old design.
  • What carries over: components, props, state and every hook, TypeScript, TanStack Query, Zustand, React Hook Form with Zod, the API and its JSON. What changes: HTML and CSS, long lists, URLs and navigation, localhost and the network, storage, permissions, the stores.
  • Expo is the framework around React Native: the CLI (npx expo …), the SDK (numbered: SDK 58), Expo Router, Expo Go (runs your project while you develop), development builds (your own Expo Go with your native modules), EAS (cloud builds, updates, submissions; a free plan).
  • Shoreline Crew is the volunteers' phone app (cleanups, details, my cleanups, check in, logbook, profile) on the same shoreline-api and types as the HQ web app.
Worked example: what React Native really draws

The lesson's CleanupCard, rendered by React Native's own renderer in Jest (no browser, no phone). This outline lists what was drawn, one native view per line:

<View>
  <View>
    <Text> "Weekend Cove Cleanup"
    <Text> "Full: join the waiting list"
    <View role="button">
      <Text> "Sign up"
  <View>
    <Text> "Family Beach Morning"
    <Text> "6 spots left"
    <View role="button">
      <Text> "Sign up"

The web preview turns the same card into HTML (the lesson's card, for the second cleanup):

<div class="css-g5y9jx r-14lw9ot r-1q9bdsx r-1cmwbt1 r-nsbfu8">
  <div dir="auto" class="css-146c3p1 r-1eiytwb r-adyw6z r-vw2c0b">Family Beach Morning</div>
  <div dir="auto" class="css-146c3p1">6 spots left</div>
  <button role="button" tabindex="0" class="css-g5y9jx r-1loqt21 r-1otgn73 r-k200y r-1eaa16r r-1xfd6ze r-ytbthy r-3pj75a" type="button">
    <div dir="auto" class="css-146c3p1 r-jwli3a r-vw2c0b">Sign up</div>
  </button>
</div>

Now the classic web habit: {title} straight inside the View, without a <Text>. npx tsc --noEmit prints nothing; running it gives the words a phone shows on its red error screen:

● the cleanup cards, as React Native draws them
  Invariant Violation: Text strings must be rendered within a <Text> component. Detected attempt to render "Weekend Cove Cleanup" string within a <View> component.
● pressing Sign up changes the button, exactly as on the web
  Invariant Violation: Text strings must be rendered within a <Text> component. Detected attempt to render "Weekend Cove Cleanup" string within a <View> component.

Read it from the top, the way you read a TypeScript error: what (an Invariant Violation), the rule (text only inside Text), the evidence (which string, inside which component). The type checker allows any child in a View; only running the code shows this.

Guided lab
  • Open labs/module-01/l1_1-how-react-native-works/TASKS.md and the test file src/lab.test.tsx first: the tests say what each component must show.
  • Once for the whole course: npm install in shoreline/labs (the setup guide). Then a terminal in the starter folder: npm test (four failures). Read the first one from the top.
  • Task 1: the site's name in a <Text> with accessibilityRole="header". Run npm test after each change; two tests pass when it is right.
QuestionYour answer
Which tool found the loose text: npm test or npm run typecheck?
What does accessibilityRole="header" do for a screen reader user?
Task 2: why does onClick type-check and still do nothing?
  • Done when npm test says Tests: 4 passed, 4 total and npm run typecheck and npm run lint print nothing after their two > lines. Finished early? npm run web for a look.

A) tsc reports that onClick does not exist on Pressable
B) The phone shows a red error screen
C) Nothing happens when a finger presses it, and no tool complains
D) It works exactly like onPress

A) A CSS Module for the event cards
B) A helper that saves the session in localStorage
C) An <img src> for the site photos

D) Expo Router

1.2

Your Toolchain

By the end you can
  • Set up Node.js, VS Code with Expo Tools and the shoreline-api for mobile development
  • Create an Expo SDK 58 project with create-expo-app and explain what each of its files is for
  • Run the app on a phone with Expo Go or in the web preview, and choose between Expo Go, an emulator and the web preview
  • Use the development loop: Metro, Fast Refresh, reload, the developer menu and the error screens
  • Check the project with npx expo lint and npx tsc --noEmit, and fix the first-run surprises both tools have
Key ideas
  • The toolchain: Node.js LTS (24 or newer, with npm and npx), VS Code with Expo Tools, Git, the Expo CLI through npx (nothing global), Metro, Expo Go on your phone, the shoreline-api. Android Studio and Xcode are optional.
  • Three ways to run: Expo Go on a phone (the real thing; the course default), an emulator or simulator (optional; the iOS Simulator needs a Mac), the web preview (layout, text, data; not native look, permissions or the camera).
  • Metro transforms (types removed, not checked), bundles per platform, serves on 8081, watches and caches (--clear). The QR code carries exp:// and your computer's network address; the web preview is http://localhost:8081 on this computer only. Fast Refresh keeps state; r reloads; m opens the developer menu.
  • The phone must reach your computer: the same Wi-Fi, one that allows device-to-device traffic, the firewall allowing Node, iPhone Local Network on; WSL needs PowerShell or mirrored networking; otherwise the web preview.
  • A new project: npx create-expo-app@latest shoreline-crew, then npm run reset-project (answer n). "main": "expo-router/entry"; app.json is the app's identity; src/app/ holds routes only; install Expo packages with npx expo install.
  • Two checks before any task is done: npx expo lint (the first run sets up ESLint, then a CommandError: run it again) and npx tsc --noEmit (needs one npx expo start on a fresh template). Both print nothing when the project is clean.
Worked example: a typo the phone ignores

One typo in src/app/index.tsx, styles.tagLine instead of styles.tagline. Metro bundles it, Fast Refresh shows it, and the tagline silently loses its size and color: no error on the phone or in the web preview. The type checker names it before anything runs:

npx tsc --noEmit
src/app/index.tsx:9:27 - error TS2551: Property 'tagLine' does not exist on type 'Readonly<{ container: { flex: number; alignItems: "center"; justifyContent: "center"; gap: number; padding: number; }; title: { fontSize: number; fontWeight: "bold"; color: string; }; tagline: { fontSize: number; color: string; }; }>'. Did you mean 'tagline'?

9       <Text style={styles.tagLine}>Many hands, cleaner sands.</Text>
                            ~~~~~~~


Found 1 error in src/app/index.tsx:9

Read it in four moves. Where: src/app/index.tsx:9:27 = file, line 9, column 27. What: the code TS2551 and the message, with a suggestion (Did you mean 'tagline'?). Why it knows: StyleSheet.create is fully typed, so the type lists every style you defined. The line: printed with ~~~~~~~ under the problem. Fixed, npx tsc --noEmit and npx expo lint both print nothing at all: no news is good news.

Guided lab
  • Open labs/module-01/l1_2-your-toolchain/TASKS.md. A terminal in starter (nothing to install: the same shared packages as Lab 1.1).
  • Task 1: npm test (one test: the tagline has no size or color), then npm run typecheck (the same mistake, named before it runs). Fix it.
  • Task 2: all tests pass; npm run lint reports one error. Fix the component, not the test.
QuestionYour answer
Which tool found the style typo first? What would a user on a phone have seen?
Which rule did the linter quote, and why did no test notice?
Stretch: which of the handout's two bugs could tsc find before you ran anything?

Self-check
Q7
Expo Go shows Shoreline Crew. You change styles.tagline to styles.tagLine and save. What does the phone show?
A) A red error screen naming index.tsx
B) Nothing changes until you scan the QR code again
C) Metro refuses to bundle the file
D) The tagline, without its size and color
Q8
npx tsc --noEmit prints this in shoreline-crew. Which change fixes it?
src/app/index.tsx:9:27 - error TS2551: Property 'tagLine' does not exist on type 'Readonly<{ … }>'. Did you mean 'tagline'?

9       <Text style={styles.tagLine}>Many hands, cleaner sands.</Text>
                            ~~~~~~~


Found 1 error in src/app/index.tsx:9
A) Change styles.tagLine to styles.tagline on line 9
B) Set "strict": false in tsconfig.json
C) Run npx expo start --clear
D) Run npx expo install --fix
Q9
Phone and laptop are on the same home Wi-Fi, Metro is running, and Expo Go on an iPhone cannot connect. What do you check first?
A) Reinstall Node.js
B) Delete node_modules and run npm install
C) Settings > Privacy & Security > Local Network: Expo Go must be on
D) Press r in the Metro terminal

B) Run npx expo lint again
C) Delete eslint.config.js and start over

A) To your computer, where the API runs

Glossary

TermMeaning
React NativeReact for iPhone and Android apps: components become the platform's own native views
native viewA building block of the phone's own interface (Android views, iOS UIView); there is no DOM
core componentsView, Text, Image, Pressable, TextInput, ... (instead of HTML elements)
StyleSheet.createTyped style objects instead of CSS: camelCase names, numbers without units, no cascade
HermesThe JavaScript engine inside the app
JSI, Fabric, native modulesJavaScript calling native code directly / the renderer that makes native views / the phone's features for JavaScript
New ArchitectureReact Native's current (and only) architecture; "the bridge" was the old one
ExpoThe framework around React Native: CLI, SDK, Expo Router, Expo Go, development builds, EAS
Expo SDKA numbered set of native modules released together (the course uses SDK 58)
Expo GoA free phone app that runs your project while you develop it
development buildYour own Expo Go-style app with exactly your project's native code (Lesson 9.2)
EASExpo Application Services: cloud builds, updates and store submissions
MetroExpo's bundler and development server (:8081): transform, bundle, serve, watch, cache
Fast RefreshSaved changes appear on every connected device in a second or two, state kept
web previewThe app in a browser, rendered as HTML by react-native-web (press w)
Expo SnackAn online editor that runs React Native in a browser (Lesson 1.1)
shoreline-apiThe course's mock back end with sample data, at http://localhost:4000/api
AGENTS.mdThe template's notes for AI coding assistants: npx expo install, lint and typecheck before done
Instructor Kit · Participant Workbook

Session 2: From the HQ to the Phone

Your workbook for Session 2: a warm-up on Session 1, the key ideas of Lesson 1.3, the copy/rewrite table, the guided lab, predict-then-check practice, a self-check, and your exit ticket.

Lesson 1.3 Session 2 · 3 hours Name: ____________________

Session Goals

  • Sort web code into "copy as it is" and "rewrite for the phone", with a reason for each.
  • Find browser-only code the type checker accepts, and say why it accepts it.
  • Read Expo's TypeScript settings and know why the course keeps TypeScript 6.0.
  • Give the app its API address with an EXPO_PUBLIC_ variable and know when the value is read.
  • Make the app's first real, checked request to the shoreline-api.

Warm-up: Your Toolchain

From memory, in pairs. Answers come out loud in a few minutes.

W1

Lesson

1.3

From the HQ to the Phone

By the end you can
  • Decide which code from a React web app carries over to React Native unchanged, which must be rewritten, and explain why
  • Spot browser-only code (the DOM, sessionStorage, window.location, import.meta.env) that the type checker may not catch
  • Read Expo's TypeScript settings and explain how they and TypeScript 6.0 differ from the web project's
  • Give the app its API address with an EXPO_PUBLIC_ environment variable, and explain when the value is read
  • Set up the shared-code folders and make a typed, checked request whose result appears on the screen
Key ideas
  • The rule: code that only works with data copies over; code that touches the browser gets rewritten. Copied: types.ts (byte for byte), the type guards, ApiError, describeError, formatDate, getAvailability. Rewritten: the API address, the session (4.3), components and CSS (Module 2 on).
  • tsc does not know you are on a phone: Expo's tsconfig loads "lib": ["DOM", "ESNext"] for the web preview, so window, document and sessionStorage type-check and fail on a phone. tsc DOES catch import.meta.env (TS2339) and missing files (TS2307). Search copied code yourself.
  • Same fetch, different failures: on phones Expo replaces fetch (expo/fetch); with no answer it throws a FetchError ("fetch failed: …"), not the browser's TypeError. The phone's request() wraps any failed fetch in a NetworkError, and describeError checks for that.
  • Expo's TypeScript settings: one short tsconfig.json extending expo/tsconfig.base, strict, @/* → ./src/* (imports without .ts). SDK 58 pins TypeScript 6.0: same checks as 7.0, and the one npx expo lint's TypeScript tools support.
  • EXPO_PUBLIC_ variables: .env.example (in Git) documents, .env.local (not in Git) holds your value; read as process.env.EXPO_PUBLIC_API_URL, copied into the bundle when Metro builds it: restart with --clear after a change, write the full name with a dot (destructuring is a lint error and leaves the address out), never secrets.
  • The address today: http://localhost:4000 in the web preview. On a phone localhost is the phone, so Expo Go shows "Could not reach the server" until Lesson 5.1.

formatDate, getAvailabilityIntl is in Hermes

288:// sessionStorage: kept when the page reloads, gone when the tab closes, never shared with other tabs
291:// What sessionStorage holds, checked: null when nothing (or not a session) is saved

315:    sessionStorage.removeItem(sessionKey)
317:    sessionStorage.setItem(sessionKey, JSON.stringify(session))
338:    return window.location.origin

Every one of those lines type-checks, because Expo's settings load the browser's types for the web preview (npx tsc --showConfig):

        "lib": [
            "dom",
            "esnext"
        ],

On a phone they fail while the app runs. The habit: search every copied file for window, document, Storage and import.meta.

Lesson

2.1

View, Text and StyleSheet

By the end you can
  • Build screens from View and Text, and say which HTML habits they replace
  • Write styles with StyleSheet.create: camelCase names, numbers instead of px, no shorthands, and know which mistakes the type checker catches
  • Explain why styles do not cascade in React Native, and use the one place they do: a Text inside a Text
  • Combine styles with style arrays, where later entries win and false entries are skipped
  • Describe how fonts, font weights, line heights and the person's text size differ between Android, iOS and the web preview
  • Keep the app's colors and spacing in a theme file and build reusable, typed AppText and EventCard components
Key ideas
  • Two building blocks: View is a box (size, background, border, padding, layout; no text); Text shows text, and only Text can. Headings are Text with accessibilityRole="header". A string straight in a View is the red screen on a phone; the web preview forgives it.
  • Styles are objects: camelCase, plain numbers of density-independent pixels (dp / points; 16 is about the same size on every phone), percentages as strings, no shorthands (borderWidth + borderColor, paddingVertical / paddingHorizontal), lineHeight is a size, no className, selectors or CSS variables. Colors are the same strings.
  • StyleSheet.create names your styles, keeps them in one place and type-checks each one. Loud: fontSize: '16px' (TS2322), border (TS2345), a misspelled property or style name. Silent: padding: '16px', text styles on a View, lineHeight: 1.5, and className in a project with Expo's generated types.
  • No cascade: a style stays on its component. The one exception: a Text inside a Text inherits the outer Text's text styles and changes only what it sets (the bold spots number). Hence a shared text component, AppText.
  • Style arrays: style={[a, b && c, props.style]} merges left to right, later entries win, false / null / undefined are skipped. AppText: base, then variant, then the screen's style.
  • Text per platform: system fonts (San Francisco, Roboto; the browser's in the web preview), fontWeight falls back to the closest weight, line height about 1.5 × the size, and text grows with the person's settings (allowFontScaling true): never a fixed height on text. Theme: brand colors and roles named by their job in src/theme/, ready for dark mode (Lesson 2.5).
Worked example: loud and silent habits

Six CSS habits, each typed into the finished app and followed by npx tsc --noEmit (fill in the middle column during the demo):

You writenpx tsc --noEmitWhat happens
fontSize: '16px'Sizes are numbers
border: '1px solid #e4dccb'No shorthands: borderWidth, borderColor
padding: '16px'Web preview: 16 px; a phone should ignore it
color, fontSize in a View's styleA View draws no text and passes nothing on
lineHeight: 1.5Lines 1.5 units tall: a sliver
<View className="card">Expo's web types allow it; a phone ignores it

The loud ones say exactly where. fontSize: '16px', cut to what you read first:

npx tsc --noEmit
src/components/AppText.tsx:20:5 - error TS2322: Type 'string' is not assignable to type 'number'.
20     fontSize: '16px',
       ~~~~~~~~
Found 1 error in src/components/AppText.tsx:20

Read it from the top: the file and line, the type expected (number), the tildes under the property. A very long first line (like border's TS2345) is not a big problem: skip to the code frame. And the silent ones? Jest with React Native's renderer shows what a Text really gets. The View has the color; the Text inside it gets nothing:

Heading, the View: {"padding":16,"color":"#0b3954","fontSize":20}
Heading, the Text inside it: {}

A style array, merged (false skipped, later wins), and AppText's three layers on the banner's title:

Spots, open: {"fontSize":16,"color":"#1f2a37"}
Spots, full: {"fontSize":16,"color":"#b23a22","fontWeight":"bold"}
AppText title on the banner: {"color":"#ffffff","fontSize":32,"lineHeight":40,"fontWeight":"bold"}

Glossary

TermMeaning
ViewThe box: size, background, border, padding, layout of its children; no text (android.view.View / UIView)
TextThe only component that shows text; a Text inside a Text is inline and inherits
density-independent pixelReact Native's unit (Android dp, iOS points): about the same physical size on every screen
StyleSheet.createNamed style objects in one place, each type-checked against what a View, Text or Image accepts
style array[a, b, c]: merged left to right, later entries win, false/null/undefined skipped
no cascadeA style belongs to one component; nothing flows down from a View
text inheritanceA nested Text gets the text styles of the Text around it
StyleSheet.flattenMerges a style array into one plain object (to look at what a component really gets)
theme tokens / color rolesColors named by their job (page, heading, error), not their look; one file to change
contentContainerStyleA ScrollView's style for the content that scrolls (padding, gap), next to style for the window
allowFontScalingOn by default: Text follows the person's text size setting
expo-env.d.tsWritten by the first npx expo start: loads Expo's types (they add className for the web preview)

Session 4: Flexbox the Native Way

Your workbook for Session 4: a warm-up, the key ideas of Lesson 2.2, a worked example, the guided lab, predict-then-check practice, the self-checks, Checkpoint 1 and your exit ticket.

Lesson 2.2 Checkpoint 1 Session 4 · 3 hours Name: ____________________

Session Goals

  • Say the four flexbox defaults that differ from CSS and spot them on a screen.
  • Line things up with justifyContent along the main axis and alignItems across it, in a column and in a row.
  • Size boxes with a number, a percentage, flex: 1 or their content, and keep text in a row from running off the screen.
  • Build a header row, label and value, wrapping badges and a shared Screen component.
  • Show what you can do with Modules 1 and 2 in Checkpoint 1.

Lessons

2.2

Flexbox the Native Way

By the end you can
  • Explain how React Native's flexbox differs from CSS: every View is a flex column, flex is a single number, nothing shrinks by default, and there is no grid
  • Line things up along the main and cross axes with flexDirection, justifyContent, alignItems, alignSelf, flexWrap and gap
  • Size boxes with flex, fixed widths and percentages, and keep text inside a row from running off the screen
  • Build the common phone layouts: a header row, label and value side by side, a wrapping row of badges, a centered message, a bar pinned to the bottom
  • Make a screen fill the phone with flex: 1, and say where the web preview's layout can differ from a phone's
Key ideas
  • Every View is a flex container, laid out by Yoga on Android and iOS; the web preview uses the browser's flexbox through react-native-web. No block, inline or grid.
  • Four defaults differ from CSS: flexDirection: 'column', flexShrink: 0 (nothing shrinks), alignContent: 'flex-start', and flex is one number (strings are a type error).
  • Axes: justifyContent along the main axis, alignItems across it; switching to a row swaps what they do on the screen. Children stretch across by default: a badge in a column becomes a bar (alignSelf: 'flex-start' or a row fixes it).
  • Sizes: a number (exact), a percentage string (a share of the parent), flex: 1 (the rest), nothing (the content). Text in a row gets flex: 1 (fill the rest) or flexShrink: 1 (natural width, may shrink).
  • Filling the screen: the outermost View gets flex: 1, or the background stops and a ScrollView inside cannot scroll; Shoreline Crew's Screen component does it once.
  • Patterns: header row, label and value, wrapping chips, centered message, bottom bar, equal columns, corner overlay (position: 'absolute' only for overlays).
Worked example: flex is a number, and what tsc cannot see

A teammate writes the web's shorthand in Screen's style. Metro would bundle it; the type checker refuses it before anything runs:

npx tsc --noEmit
src/components/Screen.tsx:28:5 - error TS2322: Type 'string' is not assignable to type 'number'.

28     flex: '1 1 0%', // fill the space the navigator gives the screen, not just the content's height
       ~~~~

  node_modules/react-native/types_generated/Libraries/StyleSheet/StyleSheetTypes.d.ts:514:3
    514   flex?: number | undefined;
          ~~~~

Found 1 error in src/components/Screen.tsx:28

Read it the Lesson 2.1 way: where (Screen.tsx:28:5), what (TS2322: a string where a number belongs), the line with ~~~~ under flex, and the type it comes from (flex?: number). Skip the long The expected type comes from … dump. In React Native flex is ONE number, a share of the free space: flex: 1.

The mistakes the type checker cannot see are the dangerous ones. A badge straight in a card becomes a bar (the column stretches its children); a long value in a row without flex: 1 can run off a phone's screen, while the web preview lets the Text shrink like CSS and looks fine. Only your eyes (and a phone) catch those: that is why every Text that shares a row gets flex: 1 or flexShrink: 1.

  • Task 3 (home if needed): siteTags(site) and the wrapping tag row.

Glossary

TermMeaning
YogaReact Native's flexbox engine on Android and iOS (the same on both)
main axis / cross axisThe direction children are placed in (down for a column, across for a row) / the other one
flex (React Native)One number: a share of the free space; flex: 1 = the rest
flexShrink: 0React Native's default: nothing gets smaller to fit
stretchalignItems' default: children fill the cross axis (a badge in a column becomes a bar)
alignSelfalignItems for one child, e.g. 'flex-start' to stop it stretching
flexWrap: 'wrap'Children that do not fit continue on the next line
ScreenShoreline Crew's wrapper: page color, padding, gaps and flex: 1 (+ scroll)
type guardisX(value: unknown): value is X: checks at run time and narrows the type (Lesson 1.3)

Lesson

2.3

Images, Icons, Fonts and Safe Areas

By the end you can
  • Show local and remote pictures with expo-image, give a picture from a server a size with aspectRatio, choose a contentFit, and describe it for screen readers
  • Use expo-symbols for icons that are SF Symbols on an iPhone and Material Symbols on Android and the web, with type-checked names
  • Load a custom font with useFonts while the splash screen waits, and pick font weights by family name
  • Explain safe areas and insets, and keep content clear of the status bar, the notch and the home indicator with react-native-safe-area-context
  • Replace the template's app icon, adaptive icon layers, splash screen and favicon, and say which ones you can see in Expo Go and which only in a build
Key ideas
  • expo-image (source, style, contentFit = object-fit, transition, accessibilityLabel = alt, disk cache by default) replaces React Native's Image. Every picture needs a size: a width and height, or the parent's width plus an aspectRatio; without one it is invisible, with no error. Local files are require()d with a plain string path.
  • Describe pictures with accessibilityLabel (and accessible): in the web preview, alt alone gave no alt. Shoreline Crew's site pictures come from the API with their alt text, width and height.
  • expo-symbols: SF Symbols on iOS, Material Symbols on Android and the web. Give all three names ({ ios, android, web }): a plain SF name type-checks and draws nothing on Android and the web. Both name sets are type-checked (a typo is TS2820 with Did you mean). Icons are named by job in one table; decorative icons are hidden from screen readers.
  • Fonts: the system font for body text; Outfit for headings, loaded with useFonts (per-weight imports) while the splash screen waits (preventAutoHideAsync / hideAsync); carry on with the system font if loading fails. One family per weight: never fontWeight with a custom font. Typed family names make a typo loud.
  • Safe areas: the status bar, the camera cutout, rounded corners and the home indicator cover the edges; the insets say by how much. useSafeAreaInsets() (or the library's SafeAreaView edges, never React Native's); a screen adds only the edges nothing else covers; scrolling: top on the frame, bottom inside the content; a pinned bar pads itself. The web preview's insets are 0.
  • App icon, adaptive icon, splash screen, favicon live in app.json and assets/images/; name is what the phone shows, slug the project's id. Expo Go shows its own icon: yours comes with your own build (Module 9). npx expo-doctor checks app.json.
Worked example: three silent mistakes, made loud

Lab 2.3's starter has three mistakes. None of them is an error on a phone: a picture that is 0 tall, an icon that is missing on Android, a button under the home indicator. The tests find them (each failure's first line):

● Task 1: the picture is one element a screen reader stops on, with the beach described
  expect(instance).toHaveProp("accessible", true)
● Task 1: the picture has a size: the card's width and the picture's own proportions
  expect(instance).toHaveStyle()
● Task 2: every terrain has an SF Symbol AND a Material Symbol for Android and the web
  expect(received).toEqual(expected) // deep equality
● Task 3: on a phone with a home indicator, the button stays above it
  expect(instance).toHaveStyle()

And the type checker finds one more before anything runs, a misspelled Material Symbol:

npm run typecheck
src/terrainIcons.ts:12:36 - error TS2820: Type '"grid_veiw"' is not assignable to type '"function" | "transform" | "opacity" | "elevation" | "filter" | "height" | "start" | "margin" | "padding" | "label" | "light" | "accessible" | "style" | "search" | "repeat" | "anchor" | ... 4039 more ... | undefined'. Did you mean '"grid_view"'?

Read it from the end of its first line: Did you mean '"grid_view"'? The same mistake also shows up in App.tsx, where the table is used; fix it in the table and both errors go. The lesson's Icon.tsx did exactly the same (line 9, then line 26).

Glossary

TermMeaning
expo-imageExpo's picture component: source, a size, contentFit, transition, a disk cache
aspectRatioWidth divided by height: with a width from the parent it gives a height before the picture arrives
contentFitHow a picture fills its box: 'cover' (default), 'contain', 'fill', 'none', 'scale-down' (CSS object-fit)
accessibilityLabelWhat a screen reader says; the web preview's alt
SF Symbols / Material SymbolsApple's and Google's icon sets; expo-symbols draws the right one per platform
useFontsexpo-font's hook: registers font files, returns [loaded, error]
splash screenThe picture shown while an app starts; kept up with preventAutoHideAsync() until the fonts are ready
safe areaThe part of the screen nothing covers
insetThe distance from an edge of the screen to the safe area (useSafeAreaInsets())
status bar / home indicatorThe clock and battery at the top / the gesture bar at the bottom, drawn on top of the app
adaptive iconAndroid's two-layer icon, cut into the phone's own shape
npx expo-doctorChecks the project's configuration (app.json, versions)

Lesson 2.4 Session 6 · 3 hours Name: ____________________

Session Goals

  • Make anything tappable with Pressable, and show the press at the touch.
  • Give every control a 48 target, a role, a label and a state for screen readers.
  • Build a controlled TextInput with onChangeText and choose its keyboard.
  • Keep taps working while the keyboard is open, and put it away at the right moments.
  • Use a Switch with onValueChange.

Warm-up: Lesson 2.3

W1

W2
Icon's table has date: 'calendar'. tsc prints nothing. What is wrong?
W3

Lesson

2.4

Touch and Input

By the end you can
  • Make anything tappable with Pressable, explain its press lifecycle (press in, press out, press, long press), and show press feedback with a style function
  • Give every control a finger-sized touch target with minHeight and hitSlop, and a role, a label and a state for screen readers
  • Build a controlled TextInput with onChangeText, and choose the keyboard and its behavior with inputMode, enterKeyHint, autoCapitalize and autoCorrect
  • Put the keyboard away at the right moments, and keep taps working while it is open
  • Use Switch for an on/off choice and know which other controls React Native has built in
Key ideas
  • Pressable reports the whole touch: onPressIn, onPressOut, onPress (a complete tap), onLongPress (500 ms; a handled long press is not also a tap). Sliding off cancels. Feedback with a style function ({ pressed }) => [...], at the touch. onClick type-checks and never runs.
  • Targets: 48 for fingers (Apple 44 pt, Android 48 dp): minHeight, never height; hitSlop enlarges the touch area (the web preview ignores it).
  • Screen readers: accessibilityRole, accessibilityLabel (an icon-only button needs one), and state with aria-* names (aria-expanded, disabled).
  • TextInput: controlled with value + onChangeText (a string); no <label>: accessibilityLabel; a placeholder is not a label. inputMode, enterKeyHint, autoCapitalize, autoCorrect, autoComplete, secureTextEntry, onSubmitEditing. Numbers are still strings.
  • The keyboard: the return key ends a one-line field; keyboardDismissMode="on-drag"; Keyboard.dismiss(); keyboardShouldPersistTaps="handled" so the first tap reaches a button. Only a phone shows it.
  • Switch: value + onValueChange (onChange gets an event); colors are props. Other controls: ActivityIndicator and RefreshControl (3.1), Alert, modal routes (4.2); no built-in slider or date picker. Derived state: the filtered list is computed on every render.
Worked example: a finger, not a mouse

The lesson's PressLog, pressed like a finger (in Jest, with React Native Testing Library's user.press and user.longPress):

Glossary

TermMeaning
PressableReact Native's one component for "this can be tapped"
onPressIn / onPressOut / onPress / onLongPressFinger down / up or slid off / a complete tap / held 500 ms
press feedbackA visible change at the touch: a style function ({ pressed }) => ...
touch targetThe area a finger can hit: 48 in Shoreline Crew
hitSlopExtra touch area around a control, outside what is drawn
accessibilityRole / accessibilityLabelWhat kind of control it is / what a screen reader calls it
aria-expandedOpen or closed, for screen readers (state, with the web's name)
onChangeTextTextInput's callback with the new text as a string
inputMode / enterKeyHintWhich keyboard / what the return key says
keyboardShouldPersistTapsWhether a tap while the keyboard is open reaches a button ('handled')
SwitchThe native on/off toggle: value + onValueChange
derived stateValues computed from state on every render instead of stored

W2

Lesson

2.5

Responsive, Platform-Specific and Themed UI

By the end you can
  • Read the window's size with useWindowDimensions, choose a layout with breakpoints in density-independent pixels, and explain why Dimensions.get at the top of a file goes stale
  • Handle real platform differences with Platform.OS, Platform.select, platform-only props and platform files (.ios.tsx, .android.tsx, .web.ts), and know when not to
  • Follow the phone's light or dark setting with useColorScheme, and design a dark set of color roles that passes the same contrast checks as the light one
  • Build a typed theme: palettes TypeScript keeps complete, a useTheme hook, and styles that are made once per theme
  • Give the navigator and the status bar the theme's colors, and fix the web preview's server-drawn first page
Key ideas
  • useWindowDimensions returns the window's width, height, fontScale and scale (dp) and re-renders when it changes; the window, not the device. Breakpoints by width (Shoreline Crew: 700); flex, percentages and maxWidth first. Dimensions.get at the top of a file goes stale.
  • Platform: Platform.OS, Platform.select (default), platform-only props (ios_…, android_…), platform files (.ios, .android, .web): tsc checks the plain file; keep variants tiny and same-typed. Size and features before platform.
  • Dark mode: app.json userInterfaceStyle: "automatic" (Android needs expo-system-ui, per Expo's docs), useColorScheme(), a dark value for every role: no pure black, lighter accents, the same contrast rules; some colors stay on purpose.
  • A typed theme: lightColors decides the roles; darkColors … satisfies ThemeColors; useTheme() for colors in props; createThemedStyles for style sheets (two sets made once). Never a hook at the top of a file.
  • Navigator and status bar: ThemeProvider with a navigation theme from the roles; StatusBar style light on dark. Web preview: server-drawn first page; useColorScheme.web.ts starts as 'light' (useSyncExternalStore's server snapshot).
  • Wide screens: useColumns() (700), rows of flex: 1 cells with an empty cell for a short row, content maxWidth: 960, alignSelf: 'center'.

Session 8: FlatList, SectionList and Big Lists

Your workbook for Session 8: the key ideas of Lessons 3.1 and 3.2, worked examples, the guided labs, predict-then-check practice, a self-check for each lesson, the Part 1 project brief and rubric, and your exit ticket.

Lessons 3.1–3.2 Session 8 · 3 hours Name: ____________________

Session Goals

  • Explain what a FlatList draws that a ScrollView does not, and why its keys come from the data.
  • Give a list every state it needs: loading, failed with Try again, nothing matches, refreshing; and items under sticky headings.
  • Explain FlashList's recycling, and keep each row's state with the right item.
  • Load a list page by page that asks once, stops after a failure and never shows an item twice.
  • Start the Part 1 project, Fix-It Tables for the Alder Row Repair Café, with TODO A done.

Lessons

3.1

FlatList and SectionList

By the end you can
  • Explain why a FlatList beats a ScrollView with map for lists, and what "drawing only what is near the screen" means
  • Build a typed FlatList: data, renderItem, a stable keyExtractor, a header, and columns with numColumns
  • Show the right thing when a list has no items: loading, a failed load with a retry button, or "nothing matches"
  • Add pull to refresh with refreshing and onRefresh, and know what each platform (and the web preview) does with it
  • Group items under headings with SectionList and sticky section headers
Key ideas
  • Virtualization: a ScrollView draws every child; a FlatList draws the rows near the screen (windowSize 21: about ten screens each way) and more as you scroll. A row thrown away is drawn again from its data.
  • FlatList's three must-haves: data, renderItem ({ item, index }, typed ListRenderItemInfo<Card>), keyExtractor (a STRING from the data's id; without it React Native quietly uses the index).
  • The list is the screen's scroll view: {...screen} from useScreenScroll(); never a list inside a vertical ScrollView. The header is an ELEMENT, not an inline component.
  • Columns: numColumns with key={columns} (a list cannot change its columns), columnWrapperStyle only with more than one column, cells as a share of the row.
  • Empty is several states: ListEmptyComponent shows loading, failed (Try again) or nothing matches; the header shows in every state. Pull to refresh = refreshing + onRefresh, false again on success and failure; the web preview has no gesture.
  • SectionList: sections of { title, data }, renderSectionHeader, stickySectionHeadersEnabled set explicitly (iOS-only by default) and a heading with a background; no columns.
Worked example: a key, and two mistakes no checker sees

A key that is not a string, in the finished Lesson 3.1 screen (keyExtractor={(card) => card.event.capacity}):

npx tsc --noEmit
src/app/index.tsx:178:7 - error TS2322: Type '(card: Card) => number' is not assignable to type '(item: Card, index: number) => string'.
  Type 'number' is not assignable to type 'string'.

178       keyExtractor={(card) => card.event.capacity}
          ~~~~~~~~~~~~

  node_modules/react-native/types_generated/Libraries/Lists/FlatList.d.ts:107:3

          ~~~~~~~~~~~~

Found 1 error in src/app/index.tsx:178

Read it in three moves. What: the second line, Type 'number' is not assignable to type 'string', names the problem in keyExtractor. Where the rule lives: the indented part points into React Native's FlatList.d.ts; you never edit it. The fix, in your file: a key from the data's identity, card.event.id (capacity is not unique even as a string).

And two mistakes no checker sees. tsc and npx expo lint print nothing for either; only running them shows them (here in Jest, React Native's own FlatList):

expect(instance).toHaveDisplayValue()

Expected instance to have display value:
  lo
Received:
  l
Invariant Violation: Changing numColumns on the fly is not supported. Change the key prop on FlatList when changing the number of columns to force a fresh render of the component.

3.2

Big Lists, Fast

By the end you can
  • Explain how FlashList's recycling differs from FlatList's virtualization, and measure both in the web preview
  • Move a FlatList to FlashList 2 and adapt what is different: spacing inside the cells, no padding on the list's own style, item types and column spans
  • Keep each item's state with the right item when its cell is recycled, with useRecyclingState
  • Load a list page by page from the API's X-Total-Pages with onEndReached, with loading, failed and end-of-list footers that never ask twice or loop
  • Show items under headings in one FlashList by flattening sections into rows with sticky header indices
Key ideas
  • Recycling: FlatList unmounts rows that leave its window and creates new ones; FlashList keeps them in pools by item type and draws them again with new data. JavaScript only in version 2 (Expo Go runs it); no estimatedItemSize any more.
  • Measure, don't guess: rows created, rows in the page and JS frames per second (FlashList's useBenchmark / useFlatListBenchmark), Chrome's CPU throttling for a cheaper phone; the web preview says nothing reliable about a phone's native side: real phones and release builds have the final word.
  • Moving to FlashList: spacing inside the cells (no content gap, no columnWrapperStyle), no padding on the list's own style (a View around it), no key={columns} needed.
  • Row state travels with a recycled row: useRecyclingState(initial, [id]), or keep it in the screen; never a key on the row inside renderItem.
  • Infinite scroll: page 1, then the next page from onEndReached only when idle; the API's X-Total-Pages to know when to stop; drop known ids; footers for loading more, failed (Try again) and the end.
  • Headings in one FlashList: flatten sections into heading and item rows, getItemType, stickyHeaderIndices, overrideItemLayout spans so headings cross columns.
Worked example: the paging headers and the rules

What the app reads to page: the second page of upcoming cleanups, five at a time (the headers only):

curl -s -D - -o /dev/null "http://localhost:4000/api/events?when=upcoming&page=2&perPage=5"
HTTP/1.1 200 OK
cache-control: no-store
content-type: application/json; charset=utf-8
link: <http://localhost:4000/api/events?when=upcoming&page=1&perPage=5>; rel="first", <http://localhost:4000/api/events?when=upcoming&page=1&perPage=5>; rel="prev", <http://localhost:4000/api/events?when=upcoming&page=2&perPage=5>; rel="last"
vary: Origin
x-total-count: 8
x-total-pages: 2
Date: (the time of your request)
Connection: keep-alive
Keep-Alive: timeout=5
Transfer-Encoding: chunked

x-total-count: 8 and x-total-pages: 2: eight cleanups, two pages. The app asks for the next page only while page < totalPages, never by guessing from how many came back. The header names come out in lowercase, which HTTP allows.

And the three rules that make it shippable, in the finished screen's code:

shoreline-crew/src/app/index.tsx
  // The list is near its end. Only ask when nothing is on its way and the last page did not fail:
  // after a failure, the Try again button asks (or the list would ask again and again)
  function onEndReached() {
    if (more.status === 'idle') {
      loadNextPage();
    }
  }

Part 1 Project: Fix-It Tables

The brief

Client: the Alder Row Repair Café, a fictional neighborhood repair café: volunteer fixers at four tables (electronics, clothing and bags, bikes, furniture), neighbors bringing broken things, a numbered paper tag on each. Contact: Rosa Delgado-Finch, the café's volunteer coordinator. The app: today's repair queue for the fixers, searchable by item or name, finished repairs hidden on request, grouped by table, readable in dark mode, one press to mark a repair done. Data: local, src/data/tickets.ts (twelve tickets): no API. Folder: labs/projects/part1-alder-row-repair-cafe/starter (the brief is TASKS.md).

Csrc/components/TicketCard.tsxthe ticket card: a heading and its tag, a status Badge, the optional fixer line, a Show problem toggle with accessibilityState, Mark fixed while in repair
Dsrc/app/index.tsxthe queue screen: a FlatList that is the whole screen, a header element (search, two switches, the count), two columns at 700 wide, the empty state, Mark fixed in state, and the grouped SectionList

Glossary

TermMeaning
FlatListReact Native's list: draws the rows near the screen (virtualization)
virtualizationDrawing only what is near the screen, more as you scroll, throwing away rows far behind
keyExtractorItem → key, a string from the data's identity (an id)
ListHeaderComponent / ListEmptyComponent / ListFooterComponentWhat scrolls above the items / shows when there are none / below them (an element or a component; undefined, not null)
numColumnsItems side by side in rows; FlatList needs key={columns} when it changes
pull to refreshrefreshing + onRefresh (RefreshControl behind them); no gesture in the web preview
SectionListItems under headings from sections; stickySectionHeadersEnabled
FlashListShopify's list (@shopify/flash-list 2.3.2): recycles rows instead of throwing them away
recyclingA row that leaves the screen is reused for a row coming in, with new data
getItemTypeSeparate pools of spare rows per kind (heading, card)
useRecyclingStateuseState that resets when its dependencies change (a recycled row's new item)
infinite scrollThe next page loads when the list nears its end (onEndReached)
X-Total-PagesThe API's header: how many pages there are
stickyHeaderIndicesThe positions of the headings FlashList pins at the top

Lessons

4.1

File-Based Routing

By the end you can
  • Explain how Expo Router turns the files in src/app into screens and addresses, including layouts, index, dynamic [id] segments and +not-found
  • Configure a stack and its header bar in three places: for every screen, for one route in the layout, and from inside a screen once its data has loaded
  • Move between screens with Link (including asChild on your own button) and with the imperative router, and choose push or replace
  • Read a route's params with useLocalSearchParams, typed from the route, and explain why they are always strings
  • Use typed routes to catch broken links with tsc, and know when Metro must run to refresh them
Key ideas
  • Files are screens: src/app/index.tsx is /, cleanups/[id].tsx is /cleanups/<any id>, _layout.tsx wraps the screens of its folder (no address), +not-found.tsx catches everything else. Only route files belong in src/app.
  • The stack: push puts a screen on top, back pops it, and the screen underneath keeps its state. On phones it is a native stack: each platform draws its own header and back button (iPhone: chevron with words, swipe from the edge; Android: arrow, system back).
  • Screen options at three levels: <Stack screenOptions> for every screen, <Stack.Screen name="…"> in the layout for one route, <Stack.Screen options={{ title }} /> inside a screen for loaded data.
  • Link and the router: a Link (with asChild on your own Button) for "go there"; router.push, replace, back and canGoBack() when code decides. Opened from its address, a screen has nothing underneath.
  • Params are strings from the address: name the route, useLocalSearchParams<'/cleanups/[id]'>(), and handle an id that names nothing (the API's 404).
  • Typed routes: Metro writes .expo/types/router.d.ts; a misspelled link is a tsc error. A route added while Metro was stopped gives stale types: start Metro first.
Worked example: params, the way back and a misspelled route

The cleanup screen reads its id from the address, typed by naming the route, and leaves the right way whether or not something is underneath (the course's code after Lesson 4.1):

shoreline-crew/src/app/cleanups/[id].tsx
export default function CleanupScreen() {
  // The [id] part of the address. Always a string: it comes from the address, like a URL's path.
  const { id } = useLocalSearchParams<'/cleanups/[id]'>();
  const [load, setLoad] = useState<Load>({ status: 'loading' });
  const [attempt, setAttempt] = useState(0);
  const styles = useStyles();
shoreline-crew/src/app/cleanups/[id].tsx
  // Back where the volunteer came from; opened from a link, there may be nothing to go back to
  function leave() {
    if (router.canGoBack()) {
      router.back();
    } else {
      router.replace('/');
    }
  }

Now one letter missing in a card's Link, '/cleanup/[id]'. Metro bundles it and the app runs; the type checker names it before a volunteer taps it:

npx tsc --noEmit
src/components/EventCard.tsx:76:23 - error TS2820: Type '"/cleanup/[id]"' is not assignable to type '"/" | RelativePathString | ExternalPathString | "/_sitemap" | "/+not-found" | "/cleanups/[id]"'. Did you mean '"/cleanups/[id]"'?

76         <Link href={{ pathname: '/cleanup/[id]', params: { id: event.id } }} asChild>
                         ~~~~~~~~


Found 1 error in src/components/EventCard.tsx:76

Read it in four moves: where (src/components/EventCard.tsx:76:23), the code (TS2820), the message: the list of every address the app has, with a Did you mean, the line with ~~~~~~~~ under the problem. That list is .expo/types/router.d.ts, written by Metro: never edit it.

4.2

Tabs, Nesting and Modals

By the end you can
  • Build a tab bar with Expo Router's JavaScript Tabs, with an icon, a title and themed colors for each tab, and explain how it differs from the template's NativeTabs
  • Use a group folder, (tabs), to nest the tab navigator inside the root stack without changing any address
  • Decide where a screen belongs: on top of the tabs (no tab bar), inside a tab (tab bar stays), or as a modal, and say which edges of the phone each one touches
  • Present a screen as a modal, close it with a button that works however it was opened, and describe how iPhones, Android phones and the web preview show it
  • Read the warnings and odd headers that a mismatch between a layout and its files produces, and fix them
Key ideas
  • Tabs switch, stacks push: a visited tab stays mounted with its state; a tab never opened is not drawn; back goes to the first tab. Import Tabs from expo-router/js-tabs (the same bar everywhere); NativeTabs is the system's own bar.
  • A group folder (tabs) shares a layout and adds nothing to the address: (tabs)/profile.tsx is /profile.
  • Navigators nest: the root Stack holds (tabs) as one screen (hide its header), next to the cleanup screen, the modal and not-found. headerBackTitle: 'Back', or a back button says "(tabs)".
  • Where a screen goes: in the root stack it covers the tabs (one address, reachable from anywhere); in a stack inside one tab the bar stays; as a modal it slides up over everything.
  • Edges: leave out every edge a bar covers: Cleanups ['top', 'left', 'right'], tab screens with a header ['left', 'right'], stack screens and modals ['bottom', 'left', 'right'].
  • A modal is a stack route with presentation: 'modal' and an address; it needs a visible Done that goes back, or replaces with / when nothing is underneath. The web preview shows it as an ordinary screen.
Worked example: the root stack and a tab that vanishes

The tab layout names one file per tab, and the root stack holds the whole group as ONE screen, its header hidden, next to the cleanup screen and the modal (the course's code after Lesson 4.2):

shoreline-crew/src/app/_layout.tsx
      <Stack
        screenOptions={{
          headerTitleStyle: { fontFamily: fonts.heading },
          // The back button's words. Left alone, it shows the previous screen's title, and under
          // the tabs that is the folder's name, "(tabs)"
          headerBackTitle: 'Back',
        }}
      >
        {/* ...and these to one. The tabs are ONE screen of this stack, with their own headers */}
        <Stack.Screen name="(tabs)" options={{ headerShown: false }} />
        {/* Until the cleanup has loaded and its screen sets its own title */}
        <Stack.Screen name="cleanups/[id]" options={{ title: 'Cleanup' }} />
        <Stack.Screen name="+not-found" options={{ title: 'Not found' }} />
        {/* A modal: it slides up over the tabs or a cleanup, instead of being pushed from the side */}
        <Stack.Screen
          name="before-you-go"
          options={{ presentation: 'modal', title: 'Before you go' }}
        />
      </Stack>

Now the tab name="my-cleanup", one letter short. npx tsc --noEmit prints nothing (screen names are plain strings). Metro, rendering the app, prints:

λ  WARN  [Layout children]: No route named "my-cleanup" exists in nested children: [ 'index', 'logbook', 'my-cleanups', 'profile' ]

and the tab bar has three tabs: My cleanups is gone. The warning lists the names that exist; fix the layout's name.

Glossary

TermMeaning
routeA screen with an address; in Expo Router, a file in src/app
layout (_layout.tsx)Wraps the screens of its folder and holds their navigator; not a screen
stackA navigator that pushes screens on top and pops them on back
dynamic route ([id])A file whose bracketed segment matches any one part of the address
paramsValues from the address, always strings: useLocalSearchParams
typed routesTypes for every address, written by Metro in .expo/types/router.d.ts
not-found route (+not-found.tsx)The screen for an address no other file matches
replaceShow a screen in place of the current one: back does not return to it
tab navigatorA bar of buttons that switches between screens that stay mounted
group ((tabs))A folder in parentheses: shares a layout, adds nothing to the address
nested navigatorsA navigator inside a screen of another (the tabs inside the root stack)
modalA stack route with presentation: 'modal': a short detour over everything
headerBackTitleThe words on a stack's back buttons
NativeTabsExpo Router's tab bar drawn by the phone itself (expo-router/native-tabs)
sitemap (/_sitemap)Expo Router's developer list of every route

Lessons

4.3

Sign-In Flows and Protected Routes

By the end you can
  • Store a session token in the phone's secure storage with expo-secure-store, explain why it is async, and say what the web preview does instead
  • Restore a saved session at startup while the splash screen stays up, so the first screen already knows who is signed in
  • Guard routes with Expo Router's Stack.Protected, and predict where the app goes when a guard changes or someone opens a guarded address
  • Build a sign-in modal that shows the API's own error messages, never keeps a failed password, and closes back to the screen that opened it
  • Sign out, and handle a 401 from the API by forgetting the session and asking the volunteer to sign in again
Key ideas
  • A token, not a password: the app sends the password once, gets a token and the user back, and keeps only those. Every later request carries Authorization: Bearer <token>.
  • Where it lives: expo-secure-store (iOS Keychain; Keystore-encrypted values on Android): async, strings only (JSON), survives restarts (so Sign out deletes it). Not AsyncStorage, never an EXPO_PUBLIC_ variable. The web preview has none: isAvailableAsync() is false, the session lives in memory only.
  • One store, one listener: the client keeps the session in memory with listeners (copied from the HQ); session.ts restores the saved copy once at startup (the splash screen waits) and saves every change. Imports point one way, or Metro warns about a require cycle.
  • useSession() = useSyncExternalStore(onSessionChange, readSession, () => null): the third function is for the web preview's server.
  • Stack.Protected guard={…} decides which screens exist. A guarded address, or a guard that turns false while its screen is open, redirects to the anchor (or redirectTo). So the sign-in modal closes first, then sets the session. A guard hides screens; the API protects data.
  • When the API forgets you: a 401 to a request that carried a token forgets the session everywhere; Profile asks GET /api/auth/me each time it comes into view (useFocusEffect) and says "Your sign-in has ended. Sign in again."
Worked example: the API's side of a sign-in

The API's side of the sign-in modal, for Theo (theo.nakamura@example.com, shoreline-demo). A wrong password and an empty form get the API's own words, which the screen shows:

HTTP/1.1 401 Unauthorized
cache-control: no-store
content-type: application/json; charset=utf-8
vary: Origin

{
  "error": "unauthorized",
  "message": "Email or password is incorrect."
}

A token the API has forgotten (after a restart) gets a 401 even from the public list of cleanups, because the request claimed to be someone:

HTTP/1.1 401 Unauthorized
cache-control: no-store
content-type: application/json; charset=utf-8
vary: Origin

{
  "error": "unauthorized",
  "message": "Your session has ended. Sign in again."
}

So the client's rule: a 401 to a request that carried a token forgets the session everywhere (setSession(null)), and session.ts's listener deletes the saved copy. A wrong password is also a 401, but from a request without a token: nothing to forget.

shoreline-crew/src/api/client.ts
    if (response.status === 401 && session !== null) {
      // The API no longer knows this sign-in (it restarted, or the session ended): forget it here too
      setSession(null);

Self-check
Q1
After Theo signs in on a phone, what does Shoreline Crew write to secure storage?
A) His email and password, so it can sign in again at the next start

4.4

Deep Links

By the end you can
  • Read a deep link such as shorelinecrew://cleanups/e08: its scheme, its path, and the file in src/app that answers it
  • Say which form of a link opens the app in a build, in Expo Go and in the web preview, and let Linking.createURL make the right one
  • Put the tabs underneath a screen opened by a link with unstable_settings.anchor, and predict where a link to a guarded screen goes
  • Share a link to a cleanup through the phone's share sheet with React Native's Share, with a fallback where there is none
  • Open links on an Android emulator, an iOS Simulator or a phone, and treat every incoming link as input from outside
Key ideas
  • A deep link = a scheme (which app: shorelinecrew, in app.json since Lesson 1.2, written into the native projects at build time) + a path (which screen: Expo Router reads it like a web address) + optional search params.
  • Three forms of one link: a build shorelinecrew://cleanups/e08; Expo Go exp://192.168.1.42:8081/--/cleanups/e08 (Expo Go owns exp; only for testing); the web preview http://localhost:8081/cleanups/e08. Linking.createURL makes the right one.
  • unstable_settings = { anchor: '(tabs)' } keeps the tabs underneath any screen a link opens, so back leads somewhere. The anchor names a screen of THAT layout; the tabs underneath load their data too. With an anchor, send people home with dismissTo, not replace.
  • Links to guarded screens follow the guard (redirect to the anchor). Every link is input from outside: ids go through encodeURIComponent, unknown ids and paths have screens, and a link only ever opens a screen.
  • Share.share() opens the share sheet: Android passes only title and message (the link goes inside the message), iOS message and url; the web preview rejects without a share sheet, so show the link in a selectable Text.
  • On a device: npx uri-scheme open "…" --android|--ios, adb shell am start …, xcrun simctl openurl booted … (quote the URL), or send yourself the link. npx uri-scheme list finds nothing until a build.
Worked example: the scheme and the anchor

The scheme in app.json (grep -n '"scheme"' app.json in shoreline-crew):

8:    "scheme": "shorelinecrew",

With it, shorelinecrew://cleanups/e08 opens src/app/cleanups/[id].tsx with id e08, in a build of the app. Expo Go answers exp://192.168.1.42:8081/--/cleanups/e08 (your address differs) and the web preview http://localhost:8081/cleanups/e08; Linking.createURL picks. The anchor puts the tabs underneath whatever a link opens:

shoreline-crew/src/app/_layout.tsx
export const unstable_settings = {
  anchor: '(tabs)',
};

Name a screen of another layout ('index', which lives inside (tabs)), and neither checker notices:

npx tsc --noEmit
npx expo lint
env: load .env.local
env: export EXPO_PUBLIC_API_URL

The error comes when Expo Router builds the stack: a blank page, and Metro prints the layout's real routes (Lab 4.4 Task 3 shows you the same message).

Glossary

TermMeaning
tokenThe API's proof of a sign-in, sent as Authorization: Bearer <token> with every request
sessionThe token and the user it belongs to (SignInResponse), kept in memory by the client
secure storageexpo-secure-store: the iOS Keychain, or Keystore-encrypted values on Android; async, strings only
useSyncExternalStoreReact's hook for a store outside React; the third function answers for the web preview's server
Stack.ProtectedA group of screens that exist only while its guard is true
anchorThe screen always at the bottom of a layout's stack (unstable_settings.anchor); also where a guard redirects
401 UnauthorizedThe API does not know who you are: a wrong password, no token, or a token it forgot
config pluginA setting in app.json that changes the native projects when the app is built
require cycleTwo files that import each other; Metro warns, and a value can be undefined
deep linkA link that opens one screen inside an app: scheme + path
schemeThe app's part of a link (shorelinecrew), registered with the phone when a build is installed
exp://…/--/…Expo Go's form of a link to your project during development
dismissToClose screens back to an address already underneath (replace only when there is nothing)
share sheetThe phone's panel for sending something to another app; React Native's Share.share()
universal links / App Linkshttps links that open the app when installed and a web page otherwise (after Module 9)

Lessons

5.1

Reaching the API from a Phone

By the end you can
  • Explain what localhost means on a computer, a phone, an Android emulator and an iOS Simulator, and pick the right API address for each
  • Open shoreline-api to your network with --host, find your computer's network address, and test it before the app is involved
  • Point the app at an address through EXPO_PUBLIC_API_URL per device, and rebuild the bundle so the change takes effect
  • Say why a phone app needs no CORS while the web preview does, and why the API's picture addresses work on every device
  • Make the HQ's typed client name the address that failed and suggest a fix, and add a connection check you can use on any device
  • Get past Windows and WSL networking, firewalls and Wi-Fi that keeps devices apart
Key ideas
  • localhost means "this device". The web preview and the iOS Simulator: http://localhost:4000. The Android emulator: http://10.0.2.2:4000. A phone on your Wi-Fi: your computer's address, such as http://192.168.1.42:4000, with the API started node server.ts --host.
  • --host makes the API listen on every address your computer has and print on your network:. Trusted networks only; Ctrl+C when done. Metro's exp://ADDRESS:8081 line shows the same address.
  • Test one link at a time: curl to the network address from the computer (Connection refused = no --host), then /api/health in the phone's browser, then the app.
  • EXPO_PUBLIC_API_URL is copied into the bundle: change .env.local, restart with npx expo start --clear. One Metro, one address. A variable set in the shell wins over the file. No /api at the end, no spaces around =.
  • No CORS on a phone: native fetch sends no Origin. The web preview belongs in the computer's own browser. Picture URLs follow the address the request used.
  • Errors that name the address: AddressError for a badly written address, NetworkError.address, advice per Platform.OS; Profile's Connection section shows the address the bundle has and checks it with an eight-second limit.
Worked example: the API on your Wi-Fi

The API started with --host prints one more line: the address other devices on your Wi-Fi use (192.168.1.42 is the course's example; yours differs):

node server.ts --host
Shoreline Crew API running at http://localhost:4000/api
  on your network:  http://192.168.1.42:4000/api
  sample sign-in: maya.okafor@example.com (organizer) or theo.nakamura@example.com (volunteer), password shoreline-demo
Press Ctrl+C to stop.

Test the road from the computer before the app drives on it:

curl http://192.168.1.42:4000/api/health
{
  "ok": true,
...
}

Then the same address in the phone's browser. If the phone shows that little JSON, the network works and anything left is in the app; if it spins, look at the Wi-Fi, the firewall and WSL. Every connection problem splits in half here. And when something fails, the client now says which address it tried and what to try on this kind of device. The function behind it, run for three addresses on three platforms:

http://localhost:4000      web      Check that shoreline-api is running, then try again.
http://localhost:4000      android  On Android, localhost is the device itself. Put your computer's network address in .env.local (10.0.2.2 on the emulator).
http://localhost:4000      ios      On a phone, localhost is the phone itself (only the iOS Simulator shares your computer's). Put your computer's network address in .env.local.
http://10.0.2.2:4000       web      10.0.2.2 works only inside the Android emulator: the web preview uses localhost.
http://10.0.2.2:4000       android  Check that shoreline-api is running, then try again. (10.0.2.2 works on the emulator, not on a phone.)
http://10.0.2.2:4000       ios      10.0.2.2 works only inside the Android emulator. Put your computer's network address in .env.local.
http://192.168.1.42:4000   web      Check that shoreline-api is running (with --host for this address), then try again.
http://192.168.1.42:4000   android  Check that shoreline-api is running with --host and that this device is on the same Wi-Fi as your computer, then try again.
http://192.168.1.42:4000   ios      Check that shoreline-api is running with --host and that this device is on the same Wi-Fi as your computer, then try again.

5.2

TanStack Query on Mobile

By the end you can
  • Say which signals a browser gives TanStack Query that a phone does not, and connect focusManager to AppState and onlineManager to NetInfo
  • Explain what a "paused" query or mutation is, and show volunteers what is happening while the phone has no connection
  • Load a list page by page with useInfiniteQuery, keeping the footer states, pull to refresh and the "ask only when idle" rule from Lesson 3.2
  • Refresh a screen's data when it comes back into view, because tabs and stacks keep screens mounted
  • Sign up for a cleanup and cancel with mutations that wait for the API and invalidate what they change
  • Notice an ended sign-in (a 401) in one place for every query and mutation
Key ideas
  • A browser tells the cache two things a phone does not: coming back to the app (AppState 'active' → focusManager.setFocused, in useAppStateFocus) and the connection (NetInfo → onlineManager.setEventListener, in useOnlineManager, with isConnected !== false). Both run once, in the root layout; the web preview keeps the browser's own events.
  • Offline = paused, not failed. A query that needs to fetch offline does not try (fetchStatus: 'paused'), the cached answer stays, and it continues by itself online. Mutations pause too (in memory only: closing the app forgets them).
  • useInfiniteQuery: initialPageParam, queryFn({ pageParam, signal }), getNextPageParam (undefined = last page); hasNextPage, fetchNextPage(), isFetchingNextPage. Ask only when idle; remove duplicates when flattening; a refetch asks for every loaded page in order. Infinite options go with the infinite hook.
  • Screens stay mounted: useRefreshOnFocus(key) = useFocusEffect + refetchQueries({ queryKey: [key], type: 'active', stale: true }), first focus skipped.
  • Sign up and cancel are mutations that wait for the API (spot or waiting list), invalidate ['events'] and ['signups'] in onSettled, and return the promise. A 409 shows the API's own words.
  • One place for 401s: QueryCache and MutationCache onError mark the sign-in as ended (src/auth/ended.ts); the viewer in every private key makes the list recover as a guest.
Worked example: a sign-up, as the API sees it

Signing up, as the API sees it (Theo's token, Boardwalk and Mudflats with 9 of 16 spots left). The first POST:

HTTP/1.1 201 Created

{
  "eventId": "e09",
  "status": "confirmed"
}

The same request a second time (another phone, or a paused mutation sent after a reconnect):

HTTP/1.1 409 Conflict

{
  "error": "conflict",
  "message": "You are already signed up for this cleanup."
}

The API decides: a spot or the waiting list, and "no" with words for a second try. So Shoreline Crew waits for the answer ("Signing up…") instead of guessing, and afterwards, success or not, invalidates the two keys a sign-up changes:

shoreline-crew/src/api/mutations.ts
// A sign-up changes the spots left (on every list and cleanup screen) and your own sign-ups.
// Returning the promise keeps the mutation pending until the screens have the new answers.
function refreshSignups() {
  return Promise.all([
    queryClient.invalidateQueries({ queryKey: ['events'] }),
    queryClient.invalidateQueries({ queryKey: ['signups'] }),
  ]);
}

/** Sign up for a cleanup. Waits for the API: it decides between a spot and the waiting list. */
export function signUpMutation() {
  return mutationOptions({
    mutationFn: (eventId: string) => postSignup(eventId),
    // onSettled, not onSuccess: after a "no" (already signed up on another phone, the last spot
    // just went) the screens need the truth too
    onSettled: refreshSignups,
  });
}

['events'] reaches the paged list and every cleanup screen, ['signups'] your sign-ups: the spots line and the panel update without a line of code that knows about spots.

  • Task 1: Providers in src/Providers.tsx. Task 2: connectOnlineManager in src/online.ts (null counts as online).
  • Task 3 (at home): getNextPageParam in src/queries.ts.
QuestionYour answer

In Task 2's test, how do you know the query was paused and not failed?
Task 3: which pages does a refetch ask for with two loaded?

Q10

No QueryClient set, use QueryClientProvider to set one
A) npx expo install @tanstack/react-query

TermMeaning

Lessons

5.3

Storing Data on the Device

By the end you can
  • Choose between AsyncStorage, SecureStore and SQLite for a piece of data, and say what survives a restart, an update and an uninstall
  • Save a small setting with AsyncStorage, check it when you read it back, and apply it before the first screen appears
  • Create a SQLite table, write rows with ? values, and ask questions across them (newest first, totals)
  • Give a database a version number and a migration, so a later version of the app can change its tables safely
  • Read data from the phone through the TanStack Query cache, without waiting for a network it does not need
  • Set up the web preview for SQLite and know where it differs from a phone
Key ideas
  • Three places: AsyncStorage (text under a key, not protected: settings, flags), SecureStore (short secrets: tokens), SQLite (tables of rows you search, sort, total). All three are asynchronous.
  • The phone keeps copies, the server keeps the truth. Storage survives a force-quit and an update; uninstalls and new phones are another story (the platforms' documentation).
  • Read saved data like API data: keep only values this version knows, fall back to defaults, never reject. Load what the first frame needs behind the splash screen. Never a token or a password in AsyncStorage.
  • Four statements: CREATE TABLE (with a PRIMARY KEY), INSERT OR REPLACE, SELECT … WHERE … ORDER BY, COUNT/SUM/COALESCE. Every value goes in a ?; only your own fixed text goes in the SQL.
  • Migrations from day one: PRAGMA user_version, one step per version, in a transaction; never delete a user's database, never edit a shipped step.
  • Phone data in the cache: networkMode: 'always' (never paused offline), staleTime: Infinity + invalidate after every write. The web preview needs wasm in Metro's asset types and one tab at a time.
Worked example: the logbook's SQL on a real log

The logbook keeps a copy of the logs the server has. Theo's one sent log, as the API gives it (trimmed):

{
  "clientId": "seed-log-1",
  "eventId": "e07",
  "volunteerId": "v04",
  "bags": 1,
  "weightKg": 2.5,
  "note": "Lots of wrappers by the stairs."
}

The logbook's own SQL (src/storage/logbook.ts) on SQLite, with that log inserted twice; then the totals and the entries:

node sql-demo.mjs
{ logs: 1, pieces: 42, bags: 1, weightKg: 2.5 }
[ 'Sunset Stairs Cleanup: Lots of wrappers by the stairs.' ]

One row: the primary key (volunteer_id, client_id) is the API's own way to recognize a log. And the same run with a note built into the SQL text instead of a ? value:

Error: near "s": syntax error

The table is made by a migration with a version number, so a later version of the app can change it on every phone:

shoreline-crew/src/storage/logbook.ts
async function migrate(db: SQLite.SQLiteDatabase) {
  const row = await db.getFirstAsync<{ user_version: number }>('PRAGMA user_version');
  const version = row?.user_version ?? 0;
  if (version >= schemaVersion) {
    return;
  }
  // All or nothing: if a step fails, the file keeps its old tables and its old number
  await db.withTransactionAsync(async () => {
    if (version < 1) {
      await db.execAsync(`
        CREATE TABLE sent_logs (
          volunteer_id TEXT NOT NULL,
          client_id TEXT NOT NULL,
          event_id TEXT NOT NULL,
          event_title TEXT NOT NULL,
          event_date TEXT NOT NULL,
          pieces INTEGER NOT NULL,
          bags INTEGER NOT NULL,
          weight_kg REAL NOT NULL,
          note TEXT NOT NULL,
          recorded_at TEXT NOT NULL,
          PRIMARY KEY (volunteer_id, client_id)
        );
      `);
    }
    // A PRAGMA takes no ? values; schemaVersion is our own number, never user input
    await db.execAsync(`PRAGMA user_version = ${schemaVersion}`);
  });
}
