5.4

Mobile Forms

By the end you can
  • Connect React Hook Form to React Native text fields with useController and Controller, and explain why register is a web tool
  • Pick the keyboard for each field (inputMode, autoComplete, secureTextEntry) and know which keys a phone's number pads leave out
  • Build a return-key focus chain with enterKeyHint, submitBehavior, onSubmitEditing and setFocus
  • Keep every field visible above the keyboard with KeyboardAvoidingView, and say what differs on Android and iOS
  • Replace a keyboard with a stepper where a thumb does better, accessibly
  • Validate a debris log with Zod on the phone and send it to the API with a device clientId, in a shape Lesson 5.5 can queue
Key ideas
  • useController, never register: the form hands each field its value, onChange (→ onChangeText), onBlur, ref (focus) and error. Lesson 5.4's ControlledField wraps it; take field apart where you get it (the linter's react-hooks/refs).
  • const send = handleSubmit(submit) and () => send() for the button and the last field: handleSubmit's function wants a web event (TS2322 otherwise).
  • A keyboard per field: inputMode email / numeric / decimal, autoComplete, autoCapitalize="none", secureTextEntry, multiline + maxLength. Decimal keys may type a comma; the iPhone's number pads have no return key (the documentation).
  • Next, Next, Go: enterKeyHint, submitBehavior="submit", onSubmitEditing={() => setFocus('next')}; the last field submits; the ref also focuses the first problem.
  • Out of the keyboard's way: FormScreen = KeyboardAvoidingView (behavior="padding" on both, the header's height as keyboardVerticalOffset) around a ScrollView. A stepper beats a keyboard for small counts.
  • One id per log, made on the phone: expo-crypto's randomUUID() once when the form opens; a resend answers 200 with the saved log, never a duplicate. The schema uses the API's messages.
Worked example: the debris log's schema, and a form for one thumb

The stage's debrisLogSchema, run with Node: a log with lots in Glass and no weight, then a good one with the weight typed 2,5:

node demo/run.mts
counts.glass: Count must be a whole number from 0 to 100000.
weightKg: Weight must be a number of kilograms from 0 to 5000.
{"bags":1,"weightKg":2.5,"note":"By the stairs","items":[{"category":"plastic-bottles","count":12},{"category":"bottle-caps","count":4}]}

The messages are the API's own; the comma became a decimal point; only the kinds that were found became items. On the screen, every field connects through useController, and the return key walks the form:

shoreline-crew/src/app/sign-in.tsx
      <ControlledField
        control={control}
        name="email"
        label="Email"
        inputMode="email" // the keyboard with @ and .
        autoComplete="email"
        autoCapitalize="none"
        autoCorrect={false}
        enterKeyHint="next" // the return key says Next...
        submitBehavior="submit" // ...keeps the keyboard up...
        onSubmitEditing={() => setFocus('password')} // ...and moves to the password
      />

The submit function is made once and called with no event, by the button and by the last field's Go key:

shoreline-crew/src/app/sign-in.tsx
  // Check, then submit. Called with no arguments: React Hook Form's handleSubmit expects a web
  // form event, and a phone's press and return key send different events
  const send = handleSubmit(submit);

Glossary

TermMeaning
AsyncStorageKey-value text storage on the phone (the browser's localStorage in the web preview); asynchronous, not encrypted
SQLite / expo-sqliteA complete SQL database in one file on the phone; expo-sqlite is the JavaScript way in
table, row, columnA table holds rows that all have the same columns
primary keyWhat makes a row unique: (volunteer_id, client_id) in the logbook
? placeholderA value SQLite fills in separately: never read as SQL (prevents SQL injection)
migration / PRAGMA user_versionA numbered step that brings an older database file up to date
transactionSeveral statements that succeed or fail together
networkMode: 'always'A query or mutation that never waits for the network (for data on the phone)
WebAssembly (wasm)How the web preview runs SQLite in the browser; Metro must be told to bundle .wasm
useController / ControllerReact Hook Form's way to connect a field that is not an HTML input
enterKeyHint / submitBehaviorThe return key's label, and what the keyboard does when it is pressed
KeyboardAvoidingViewA View that adds room for the keyboard (behavior="padding", keyboardVerticalOffset)
stepper−, the number, +: a thumb control for small counts, with a box for big ones
clientIdAn id the phone makes once per log, so the API recognizes a resend

Lessons

5.5

Offline First

By the end you can
  • Explain "offline first" as an outbox: write on the device first, send when you can, and say what a paused mutation from Lesson 5.2 cannot do
  • Persist a Zustand store on a phone with persist and AsyncStorage, load it behind the splash screen, and say what differs from the web course's localStorage store
  • Send a queue safely: one log at a time, the same clientId on every try, and a different reaction to each kind of answer (sent, refused, signed out, no answer)
  • Choose the moments to send (start, sign-in, back online, back in the foreground) with the online and focus managers you already have
  • Keep each volunteer's unsent work apart on a shared phone
Key ideas
  • Offline first is an outbox: write on the device first, send when you can. Lesson 5.4's paused mutation waits in memory, which ends when the app does; today's queue is saved in AsyncStorage the moment Send is pressed.
  • Two rules: save before sending; leave the queue only after the server and the logbook both have it. Queue what a person creates (a log); let what the server decides (a sign-up) wait for the server.
  • Zustand on a phone: persist comes across from the web, with three differences: name the storage (createJSONStorage(() => AsyncStorage)), load it yourself behind the splash (skipHydration + rehydrate()), save only what should survive (partialize). merge checks saved data, as in the HQ.
  • Every answer means something: 201 and 200 done; other 4xx refused (kept with the server's words); 401 stop and sign in; no answer or 5xx: keep, count, stop. The clientId makes "no answer: send again" safe (200 with the saved log).
  • When to send: Send, start or sign-in, back online (onlineManager), back in the foreground (focusManager), Send now. The sender does nothing when it should not run, so calling it often is harmless. Not while the app is closed.
  • A shared phone: every queued log names its volunteer; only that volunteer's sign-in sends it (checked before each request); sign-out keeps the queue.
Worked example: persist with no storage

The web course's persisted store, copied to the phone with no storage option. tsc and the linter say nothing, and the web preview works (it has localStorage). Run the way the lesson ran it (Node, a window without localStorage, as on a phone):

// The web course's persisted store, copied as it was: no storage option
globalThis.window = {}; // a window without localStorage, as on a phone
const { create } = await import('zustand');
const { persist } = await import('zustand/middleware');

const useLogQueue = create()(
  persist((set) => ({ logs: [], add: (log) => set(({ logs }) => ({ logs: [...logs, log] })) }), {
    name: 'shoreline-crew-log-queue',
  }),
);
try {
  useLogQueue.getState().add({ clientId: 'c3a0f6b2' });
  console.log('add worked');
} catch (error) {
  console.log('add threw: ' + error);
}
add threw: TypeError: Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'setItem')

Read it from the top: a TypeError, reading setItem of something undefined: persist reached for a storage that is not there. On a phone, the same moment is the red screen when Send is pressed. The fix is one option: storage: createJSONStorage(() => AsyncStorage), and with it the two other phone differences: skipHydration with loadLogQueue() behind the splash (AsyncStorage answers later), and partialize (save the logs only: sending must end with the app).

  • Task 1: name the storage, save only the reports. Then Task 2: sort the answers.

6.1

Permissions Done Right

By the end you can
  • Describe the life of a permission on an iPhone, an Android phone and the web preview: not asked, granted, denied, and blocked (plus the one-time and approximate answers)
  • Ask at the moment a feature needs it, explain why first, and handle "no" and "don't ask again" without trapping the person
  • Use Expo's permission model: the permission Hooks, get…PermissionsAsync versus request…PermissionsAsync, the PermissionResponse object, and what the Hooks leave to you
  • Write the words on the phone's question with a config plugin in app.json, ask only for what you use, and check what reaches the native projects
  • Say what Expo Go, a development build and the web preview each show you about permissions
Key ideas
  • The life of a permission: not asked, granted (one-time and approximate answers count), denied (Android may ask once more), blocked (iPhone after one no, Android after two, Chrome after Never allow). Answers change in Settings at any time: check before every use.
  • Five rules (the farm gate): ask in context; explain first, with Continue (the real Allow is the phone's) and Not now; a no is an answer (everything else keeps working); say when only Settings can help; check before every use.
  • Expo's model: get…PermissionsAsync reads, request…PermissionsAsync asks if it still may, the Hook returns [response, request, get]; every answer is a PermissionResponse (status, granted, canAskAgain, expires). The Hook never asks by itself and never re-reads after Settings.
  • The project's step: permissionStep turns an answer into checking, not-asked, denied, blocked or granted (a web no is blocked); usePermission re-reads on focus; PermissionGate has one face per step and renders the feature only after a yes.
  • The words on the question: an iPhone shows the purpose string from Info.plist; Android lists permissions in its manifest. Config plugins in app.json write both; expo-location's plugin runs with default words even when unlisted: list it to use your words and false for what you do not use. npx expo config --type introspect shows the result.
  • Where you see what: Expo Go asks as itself, with its own words, and remembers answers across projects; a development build asks as Shoreline Crew, with your words (Module 9); the web preview asks as the browser, per address.
Worked example: the Hook passed uncalled

One missing pair of parentheses in DistancePanel.tsx, usePermission(Location.useForegroundPermissions). The type checker stops it:

npx tsc --noEmit
src/components/DistancePanel.tsx:43:34 - error TS2345: Argument of type '(options?: PermissionHookOptions<object> | undefined) => [LocationPermissionResponse | null, () => Promise<LocationPermissionResponse>, () => Promise<...>]' is not assignable to parameter of type 'PermissionHookResult'.

43   const location = usePermission(Location.useForegroundPermissions);
                                    ~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~

  src/components/DistancePanel.tsx:43:34
    43   const location = usePermission(Location.useForegroundPermissions);
                                        ~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~
    Did you mean to call this expression?


Found 1 error in src/components/DistancePanel.tsx:43

Read it from the bottom: "Did you mean to call this expression?" is the whole answer. The long type in the first line is the Hook itself (a function that takes options and returns a three-item tuple); PermissionHookResult is the tuple. usePermission(Location.useForegroundPermissions()) passes what the Hook returns. Without tsc, the mistake would reach the screen as a crash: Lab 6.1's starter shows it in Jest.

Glossary

TermMeaning
offline first / outboxWrite on the device first, send when the network allows; the record survives the app closing
queueThe saved list of records on their way to the server (here: debris logs in AsyncStorage)
persist (Zustand)Middleware that saves a store after every change and reads it back
createJSONStorage(() => AsyncStorage)Tells persist where to save on a phone (there is no localStorage)
hydration / rehydrate()Reading the saved state back into the store; on a phone it is async, so the app waits behind the splash
partializepersist's option for saving only part of the state
idempotent retryA resend that changes nothing: the clientId makes the server answer 200 with what it already has
useShallowCompares a selector's items, so a filtered list does not render forever
runtime permissionThe phone's own question before an app may use location, the camera, the microphone, notifications…
PermissionResponseEvery Expo permission answer: status, granted, canAskAgain, expires
canAskAgainfalse: the phone will not ask again; only Settings can change the answer (always true on the web)
pre-permission explanationThe app's own sentence before the phone's question, with Continue and Not now
purpose stringThe iPhone's usage description in Info.plist, printed in its question
config pluginCode in app.json's plugins that writes the native projects' settings at prebuild
npx expo config --type introspectPrints what the plugins will write, without creating ios/ or android/
haversineThe formula for the straight-line distance between two latitude/longitude points

Lessons

6.2

Camera and Photos

By the end you can
  • Take a photo with the phone's camera (after Lesson 6.1's permission step) and choose photos from the library (no permission), and read what the picker gives back
  • Keep photos small enough for one bar of signal: compression quality, no EXIF, JPEG instead of HEIC, and the API's limits checked before anything is sent
  • Keep a photo safe while it waits: the cache versus the app's documents, with expo-file-system's File, Directory and Paths, and a .web.ts stand-in for the web preview
  • Upload a photo from a phone to POST /api/photos and say why the old React Native FormData trick fails with SDK 58's fetch
  • Send photos offline first: queued with their log, uploaded before it, each id saved so a retry never uploads a photo twice, with a version bump for the saved queue
Key ideas
  • Two ways to a photo: launchCameraAsync (the camera: Lesson 6.1's permission step) and launchImageLibraryAsync (the library: no permission on current phones). Both answer { canceled, assets }; each asset has a uri (a file on the phone), mimeType, fileSize, width and height.
  • Photos for one bar of signal: quality: 0.6, exif: false, and on an iPhone the "compatible" JPEG instead of HEIC. shoreline-api takes JPEG, PNG or WebP of 5 MB or smaller (415 and 413 otherwise): check when a photo is ADDED.
  • Where a photo lives: the picker's file is in the cache, which the phone may empty; a photo that waits moves into the app's documents (expo-file-system's File, Directory, Paths) and is deleted once the server has it. The web preview gets photoFiles.web.ts.
  • Sending a photo: the photo itself as the body with its Content-Type (an expo-file-system File is a Blob). The old { uri, name, type } FormData part fails with SDK 58's fetch (TS2769; with a cast, "Unsupported FormDataPart implementation" inside fetch).
  • Photos in the dry bag: the queue carries { uri, type, photoId } per photo; the sender uploads photos without an id first, saves each id at once (setPhotoId), then sends the log with photoIds. A 413 or 415 makes the log refused, like a 403.
  • Saved data changes shape: persist's version and migrate give logs saved by version 0 an empty photo list, so they survive the update.
Worked example: the classic upload, and why it fails

The classic React Native upload from older answers, written into postPhoto: a FormData part made of the file's address. The type checker stops it:

// Don't: React Native's old FormData trick (a file address instead of a Blob)
const body = new FormData();
body.append('photo', { uri: photo.uri, name: 'photo.jpg', type: 'image/jpeg' });
await fetch(`${apiBaseUrl()}/api/photos`, { method: 'POST', body });
npx tsc --noEmit
src/api/client.ts:452:8 - error TS2769: No overload matches this call.
  Overload 1 of 3, '(name: string, value: string | Blob): void', gave the following error.
    Object literal may only specify known properties, and 'uri' does not exist in type 'Blob'.
  Overload 2 of 3, '(name: string, value: string): void', gave the following error.
    Argument of type '{ uri: string; name: string; type: string; }' is not assignable to parameter of type 'string'.
  Overload 3 of 3, '(name: string, blobValue: Blob, filename?: string | undefined): void', gave the following error.
    Object literal may only specify known properties, and 'uri' does not exist in type 'Blob'.

452   body.append('photo', { uri: photo.uri, name: 'photo.jpg', type: 'image/jpeg' });
           ~~~~~~

Read the overloads: append takes a string or a Blob, and the object is neither. Casting it away (as unknown as Blob) moves the error to the phone: SDK 58's fetch is Expo's own, and its code turns a FormData into bytes part by part. Run on such a form (Expo's own code, from node_modules), it says:

old way: Error: Unsupported FormDataPart implementation

Inside fetch, so the app reports "Could not reach the server" and the queue waits forever. The fix: send the photo itself as the body, with its kind in Content-Type (an expo-file-system File is a Blob).

  • Open labs/module-15/l6_2-camera-and-photos/TASKS.md and src/lab.test.tsx: the test names say the rules.
  • A terminal in starter: npm test (eight failures) and npm run typecheck (where did you see its error today?).
  • Task 1: what the picker gave back. Then Task 2: bags first, then the tally sheet.

6.3

Location and Maps

By the end you can
  • Read a phone's position as it really is: coordinates with an accuracy radius, a time, and a way to be faked, and pick the accuracy a feature needs
  • Design an on-the-day check-in: decide what proves a volunteer was there (the organizer's code, not the phone's location), and say honestly what happens offline
  • Build the check-in as a typed modal route with React Hook Form, a Zod schema and a mutation, and put the API's field message on the right field
  • Show a map with react-native-maps (a pin, a starting region, the blue dot only after a yes) and say what Expo Go, development builds and the web preview each need, including the Google Maps key for Android builds
  • Give the web preview a stand-in for a native-only library with a .web.tsx file, and hand a place to the phone's map app with a link
Key ideas
  • A position is a circle: coords.latitude, longitude and accuracy (the radius in meters), a timestamp, and mocked. The accuracy option (Lowest to BestForNavigation) trades time and battery for precision; under cliffs and between buildings circles of 50 to 500 m are normal.
  • What proves you were there: the organizer's code, checked by the server (on the day, a confirmed spot). The phone's position can be faked or far off and needs a yes to location: a hint, never a rule. Rules belong on the server.
  • Checking in with one bar: a TanStack Query mutation, not the saved queue (a late check-in can never work, a wrong code needs the volunteer now). Offline it pauses in memory and the screen says so. The API's check-in is idempotent.
  • The modal: faces before a form (guest, no confirmed spot, not today, checked in), a schema that keeps the code as text, the API's message for code on the field, and onSettled refreshing ['signups'] so the screen changes by itself.
  • Maps on a phone: react-native-maps' <MapView> with initialRegion (latitudeDelta 0.01 is about 1.1 km) and <Marker>; showsUserLocation makes an iPhone ask, so only after a yes. Expo Go needs no setup; your own Android build needs a Google Maps key (Module 9).
  • The web preview: react-native-maps stops the whole web build when imported: SiteMap.web.tsx stands in. Directions are the map app's job: mapLink per platform, opened with React Native's Linking.openURL (a new tab on the web).
Worked example: a native-only library and the web build

Lesson 6.3's map, imported where the web build sees it (the finished code with SiteMap.web.tsx removed). The web export stops:

npx expo export --platform web
env: load .env.local
env: export EXPO_PUBLIC_API_URL
Using src/app as the root directory for Expo Router.
React Compiler enabled
Starting Metro Bundler

Server rendering is enabled. Learn more: https://docs.expo.dev/router/web/server-rendering/
λ Bundled 10857ms node_modules/expo/node_modules/@expo/cli/node_modules/@expo/router-server/node/render.js (1247 modules)
Web Bundled …ms node_modules/expo-router/entry.js (1235 modules)

Metro error: (0 , r(...).codegenNativeComponent) is not a function

  TypeError: (0 , r(...).codegenNativeComponent) is not a function

Read it as "a native component was defined where there is no native": react-native-maps defines native components when it is loaded, and react-native-web has no codegenNativeComponent. Expo Router's web build loads every route to render the pages on the server, so one import stops all of them. The fix is a file name: SiteMap.web.tsx next to SiteMap.tsx, with the same props, never importing react-native-maps.

Glossary

TermMeaning
image picker (expo-image-picker)Opens the phone's own camera or photo library and returns { canceled, assets }
assetOne photo from the picker: uri, mimeType, fileSize, width, height
EXIFData inside a photo (camera, time, often the place); exif: false leaves it out
HEICThe iPhone's photo format; shoreline-api refuses it (415)
cache / documentsThe app's two folders: the phone may empty the cache, it leaves documents alone
BlobRaw data with a type; an expo-file-system File is one, so fetch can send it as a body
raw body versus multipartThe API's two upload forms: the image itself with its Content-Type, or a form with a photo field
persist version / migrateA saved store's version number, and the function that updates data saved by an older version
position accuracyThe radius in meters of the circle a phone's position probably lies in
mock locationA position set by an app or tool instead of measured; mocked catches some
check-in codeThe organizer's four digits for the day; the API checks it
idempotent requestSending it again changes nothing: a second check-in leaves the sign-up attended, with the first time
region / latitudeDeltaWhere a map starts and how much fits on screen (0.01 of latitude is about 1.1 km)
markerA pin on the map (<Marker>), with a title and a description
Google Maps API keyNeeded by your own Android builds (not Expo Go); restricted to your app
.web.tsx stand-inA file the web bundle uses instead of the phone's version, with the same exports
geo: linkAndroid's link for a place, opened by the map app

Lessons

6.4

Notifications

By the end you can
  • Tell local notifications from push notifications and choose between them: what each needs, what each can do, and why Shoreline Crew's reminders are local
  • Put notifications through Lesson 6.1's permission step with a Hook made by createPermissionHook, including Android's channels and the iPhone's question
  • Schedule, replace and cancel reminders with expo-notifications, and keep them in step with the server's sign-ups in one function that is safe to run at any time
  • Open the right screen from a tap on a notification, whether the app was running or not, and treat the notification's data as outside input
  • Say what server push would need (a development build, an EAS project, credentials, push tokens and a server), and what Expo Go and the web preview can and cannot show
Key ideas
  • Local or push: a local notification is scheduled by the app and shown by the phone's own scheduler, with no server and no signal; a push is sent by a server through Apple's or Google's push service. Known in advance (a reminder): local. Known only to a server, when it happens (a cancellation): push.
  • Asking: iPhone once; Android 13 and later asks (once more after a first no), and only once the app has a channel; the web preview uses the browser's question. createPermissionHook makes the missing Hook; Lesson 6.1's gate does the rest. The moment is the switch, never the launch.
  • A reminder: content (title, body, data), a trigger with a type (DATE, TIME_INTERVAL, DAILY, WEEKLY), an identifier. The same identifier again replaces the reminder.
  • Reminders that stay true: one function, updateReminders, reads the truth (session, switch, permission, confirmed sign-ups) and makes the phone match: cancel its own unwanted, schedule the wanted. Safe to run any time, so the app runs it often.
  • A tap: a listener while the app runs; getLastNotificationResponse() once when the tap started it. The data is outside input: a type guard, and only ever open a screen.
  • Push would need a development build, an EAS project, FCM and APNs credentials (APNs is paid), a push token per device, a server that stores tokens, and a sender that reads receipts. The course describes it and does not ship it.
Worked example: a Date as the trigger

Older examples pass a Date as the trigger. In the finished code, trigger: reminderTime(event) instead of the object:

npx tsc --noEmit
src/notifications/reminders.ts:120:9 - error TS2322: Type 'Date' is not assignable to type 'NotificationTriggerInput'.

120         trigger: reminderTime(event),
            ~~~~~~~

  node_modules/expo-notifications/build/Notifications.types.d.ts:638:5
    638     trigger: NotificationTriggerInput;
            ~~~~~~~
    The expected type comes from property 'trigger' which is declared here on type 'NotificationRequestInput'


Found 1 error in src/notifications/reminders.ts:120

Read the first line: a Date is not a NotificationTriggerInput, the type the second part points to in the library's declarations. In expo-notifications 58 a trigger says what kind of time it is: { type: Notifications.SchedulableTriggerInputTypes.DATE, date: reminderTime(event) }. (Plain JavaScript would get through with a deprecation warning; TypeScript catches it first.) Lab 6.4's starter has the same mistake in a new place.

6.5

Haptics, Sharing and Files

By the end you can
  • Choose the right haptic for a moment with expo-haptics (or none at all), and say why a buzz is never the only feedback
  • Respect the people holding the phone: an in-app switch for vibration, and the phone's own settings, on iPhone and Android
  • Write a file with expo-file-system's File and Directory, in the right folder, without breaking the web preview
  • Turn app data into a CSV file a spreadsheet opens correctly, with quoting, a byte order mark and a guard against formulas
  • Hand a file to the share sheet with expo-sharing, and explain how that differs from Lesson 4.4's text sharing on each platform
Key ideas
  • Three kinds of haptic: notificationAsync (the result of something: Success, Warning, Error), impactAsync (something touched something), selectionAsync (a value changed under a finger); on Android, performAndroidHapticsAsync plays the system's own effects (Confirm, Reject).
  • Five rules: outcomes, not presses; the same meaning every time; never the only feedback (words on screen, read by screen readers); less is more (two moments in the whole app); optional (a switch).
  • Two layers of off: the app's switch (yours, on every platform) and the phone's settings (iPhone System Haptics; Android vibration and haptics), which have the last word.
  • Writing a file: new File(...) is only an address; create(), write(), text(). Files made to be shared go in the cache. On the web there are no files: create File, Directory and Paths inside functions, never at the top of a module, and check the platform first.
  • A CSV a spreadsheet trusts: text quoted, inner quotes doubled, numbers bare, a byte order mark first, \r\n, and a ' before text that starts like a formula.
  • The share sheet: React Native's Share for words and links (Lesson 4.4); expo-sharing's shareAsync for ONE local file, with a mimeType (Android) and a UTI (iPhone). It resolves when the sheet closes, never says whether anything was shared.
Worked example: a CSV a spreadsheet trusts

The finished cell() and logbookCsv() from src/storage/logbookExport.ts, run in Node on three logbook rows (the Answer Key has the whole script). The last row's note starts with =:

"Cleanup","Date","Pieces","Bags","Weight (kg)","Note"
"Sunset Stairs Cleanup","2026-11-07",42,1,2.5,"Lots of wrappers by the stairs."
"Weekend Cove Cleanup","2026-11-14",63,2,4.1,"Bottles, cans and 2 ""lost"" flip-flops.
Café cups too."
"Tide Pool Morning Cleanup","2026-11-15",12,1,0.8,"'=HYPERLINK(""http://example.com"",""Open"")"

Every row has 6 cells: text in double quotes with each inner quote doubled, the line break inside its cell, numbers bare so they add up, and a ' before the formula so a spreadsheet shows it as text (CSV injection). Not visible: the file starts with U+FEFF, the byte order mark, and every row ends with \r\n.

Part 2 Project Kickoff

Bookmobile Stops: the brief in one page

The client: Tamarack County Library Bookmobile. Contact: Odile Varga, outreach librarian. The app: Bookmobile Stops. The folder: labs/projects/part2-tamarack-bookmobile/ (read its brief first).

A library van visits about eight stops a week. Patrons want to see where and when the van stops next, browse what is on board, and ask for a book to be held for them at a stop, often from a parking lot with one bar of signal.

IdRequirementWhich lesson(s)?What I already have from Shoreline Crew
R1Routing and tabs
R2Simple sign-in and a protected tab
R3Data from the mock API
R4Offline-safe storage
R5The form and the device feature
R6Quality
  • Rubric: each row 0 (not yet), 1 (meets), 2 (strong); 12 points. Complete = no row at 0.

Glossary

TermMeaning
local notificationScheduled by the app on the phone; the phone's scheduler shows it, with no server and no signal
push notificationSent by a server through Apple's (APNs) or Google's (FCM) push service, to a token per device
notification channelAndroid's category for notifications ("Cleanup reminders"), which the person can tune in Settings
triggerWhen a scheduled notification fires: { type: DATE, date }, TIME_INTERVAL, DAILY, WEEKLY
notification handlersetNotificationHandler: how a notification that arrives while the app is open is shown
notification responseA notification plus what the person did with it (a tap); its content.data rides along
entitlement (aps-environment)An iPhone app's declared capability to receive pushes; enableRemoteNotifications: false leaves it out
idempotent syncOne function that makes the phone match the truth; running it again changes nothing
hapticsShort, precise taps from the phone's motor; expo-haptics plays them
share sheetThe phone's panel of apps and actions a file or text can be handed to
MIME type / UTIA file's type for Android (text/csv) and for an iPhone (public.comma-separated-values-text)
CSVComma-separated values: plain text that every spreadsheet opens
byte order mark (BOM)The invisible character U+FEFF at the start of a file that tells spreadsheets the text is UTF-8
CSV injectionA cell that starts with = + - or @ run as a formula; guarded with a leading '
cache directoryThe app's folder for files the phone may throw away (Paths.cache)

Part 2 Assessment

Closed book: laptops closed, no notes, no phones, no chat assistants. 45 minutes, 20 points. Items A1 to A14: circle ONE letter (1 point each). Items A15 to A17: two or three sentences (and a line of code where it helps), 2 points each: a reason is worth more than a keyword. 14 or more of 20 points (70 %) meets the Part 2 assessment.

A1
In the Lesson 4.1 app (before Lesson 4.4's anchor), the cleanup screen's leave() runs router.canGoBack() ? router.back() : router.replace('/'). When does the replace branch run? (1 point)
A) When the volunteer presses the back arrow in the header
B) When the screen was opened straight from its address (or a link from outside): it is the only screen in the stack, so there is nothing to go back to
C) Whenever the API answers 404 for the id
D) Only on Android, which has a system back button
A2
Moving the Cleanups screen into the tab group, a learner copies src/app/index.tsx to src/app/(tabs)/index.tsx and forgets to delete the original. What does the app show? (1 point)
A) Metro stops with an error: two routes claim the address /
B) The tab bar, with the Cleanups tab listed twice
C) The root file wins: the Cleanups screen opens with no tab bar at all and a header titled index, and Metro prints no warning
D) npx tsc --noEmit reports a duplicate route
A3
Shoreline Crew's sign-in modal calls router.back() and only then setSession(session) when the API says yes. Why that order? (1 point)
A) The moment the session is set, Stack.Protected takes the sign-in screen away; if it were still open, Expo Router would send the volunteer to the first tab instead of back to the screen that opened sign-in
B) setSession throws while a modal is open
C) router.back() is what saves the token in SecureStore
D) The order does not matter: it is a style choice
A4
With unstable_settings = { anchor: '(tabs)' } in the root layout, the not-found screen still uses Lesson 4.1's <Link href="/" replace asChild>. A volunteer opens a mistyped address and presses Back to the cleanups. What happens? (1 point)
A) Metro prints an error about the anchor
B) The app closes, because nothing is left in the stack
C) The Cleanups list appears with one tab bar, exactly as intended
D) The list looks right, but the page now holds two tab bars: a copy of the tabs replaced the not-found screen on top of the tabs the anchor put underneath. dismissTo closes screens back to the existing tabs instead
A5
The school's Wi-Fi keeps devices apart, so you start Metro with npx expo start --tunnel. Expo Go now loads Shoreline Crew on your phone, but the Cleanups list says it could not reach the server. Why? (1 point)
A) The tunnel accepts only https addresses, and the API uses http
B) --tunnel switches off every EXPO_PUBLIC_ variable
C) The tunnel carries only Metro's traffic: the app's requests still go to EXPO_PUBLIC_API_URL, an address the phone cannot reach, and shoreline-api has no tunnel
D) shoreline-api must be restarted with --tunnel as well
A6
Theo spends ten minutes on the Cleanups tab, then switches back to My cleanups. In a version WITHOUT useRefreshOnFocus('signups'), what happens to his sign-ups list? (1 point)
A) Nothing asks the API: the tab stayed mounted, and neither the app's focus nor the network changed, so the list shows what was fetched ten minutes ago
B) It is fetched again, because the screen mounts again
C) Query fetches it again, because the window gained focus
D) The list is empty until he pulls to refresh
A7
The settings saved on a phone are {"theme":"sepia"} (written by an older version, or by a bug). Shoreline Crew starts. What happens? (1 point)
A) TypeScript rejects the saved value before the app starts
B) useTheme meets an unknown theme and the app shows a red error screen
C) The app clears everything in AsyncStorage
D) The settings store keeps a theme only if it is one of the three it knows, so it falls back to the default: the app opens with Phone setting chosen
A8
Why does the sign-in screen write const send = handleSubmit(submit) and onPress={() => send()} instead of the web course's habit, onPress={handleSubmit(submit)}? (1 point)
A) handleSubmit works only inside a <form>, which React Native has only on the web
B) handleSubmit(submit) returns a function typed to take a web form event; a press event and the return key's submit event are different types, so tsc rejects it. Called with no event at all, it type-checks
C) onPress cannot take a function that another function returned
D) Calling handleSubmit while rendering sends the form twice
A9
Shoreline Crew saves debris logs in a persisted queue that survives the app, but leaves sign-ups as Lesson 5.2's mutations, which wait in memory. Why? (1 point)
A) A sign-up is decided by the server (a spot or the waiting list), so the volunteer needs the answer now; the queue is for things a person creates (a log, a note, a photo)
B) Sign-ups are too big for AsyncStorage
C) Zustand can persist only one kind of item
D) Sign-ups never fail when the phone is offline
A10
Lesson 6.1's app.json entry for expo-location sets locationAlwaysAndWhenInUsePermission, locationAlwaysPermission, motionUsagePermission and locationFullAccuracyPermission to false. Why? (1 point)
A) To turn location off in the web preview
B) So that Expo Go skips the location question
C) expo-location's plugin runs even when it is not listed, with default words for every string; false removes what Shoreline Crew never uses (Always location, motion, temporary precise location), so the app asks only for what it uses
D) Without them, npx expo install expo-location fails
A11
Shoreline Crew's picker options include preferredAssetRepresentationMode: Compatible. What is it for? (1 point)
A) It asks for the photo-library permission before the picker opens
B) iPhones save photos as HEIC; this option asks iOS for its most compatible version, a JPEG, because shoreline-api answers HEIC with 415 Photos must be JPEG, PNG or WebP images
C) It makes Android photos smaller
D) It removes the location data (EXIF) from the photo
A12
A teammate writes checkInMutation with only a mutationFn. Theo types the right code; shoreline-api answers 200 and marks his sign-up attended. What does the screen do? (1 point)
A) It shows You are checked in at once
B) It shows an error, because the answer carries no new data
C) It goes back to the cleanup screen
D) Nothing changes: still the form, still the code, still Check in. The screen draws from the cached sign-ups, and nothing marked them out of date: onSettled must invalidate ['signups']
A13
When a volunteer taps a reminder, useReminderTaps reads url from the notification's content.data. Why does it pass the value through isCleanupPath() before router.push? (1 point)
A) router.push accepts only addresses that start with https://
B) A notification's data is outside input: it may be missing and its values are typed unknown; the type guard accepts only a cleanup's path, so anything else (a web address, /sign-in, a number) opens nothing
C) isCleanupPath() schedules the next reminder
D) Without it, Android would not show the notification
A14
A teammate adds const folder = new Directory(Paths.cache, 'exports') at the top level of src/storage/logbookExport.ts. On a phone it works. What happens in the web preview? (1 point)
A) The Logbook tab fails to load: importing the module is enough to throw this.validatePath is not a function (a blank tab; the exported preview answers /logbook with a 500). Create files and folders inside the functions that use them
B) Only the Share my logbook button is disabled
C) The file is written to the browser's downloads folder
D) tsc reports that Paths does not exist on the web
A15
A teammate says: "My cleanups shows guests a sign-in face, so GET /api/me/signups does not need to check the token." In two or three sentences: why is that wrong, and what does Shoreline Crew do when the API answers 401 to a request that carried a token? (2 points)
A16
This is the debris-log form after Lesson 5.5. Why is clientId made once, when the form opens, and not inside send (for example toNewLog(data, randomUUID(), new Date()))? And what does shoreline-api do when the same log arrives twice? (2 points)
shoreline-crew/src/app/log/[id].tsx
function DebrisLogForm({ event, viewer }: { event: CleanupEvent; viewer: string }) {
  // One id per log, made on the phone the moment the form opens: if the same log is sent twice
  // (a retry after a lost answer), the API recognizes it and keeps one
  const [clientId] = useState(() => randomUUID());
  ...
  // Checked by the schema first; the time is when Send was pressed, not when the form opened.
  // The log goes into the queue (saved on the phone at once), and the queue sends it if it can.
  const send = handleSubmit((data) => {
    const log = toNewLog(data, clientId, new Date());
    addLog(viewer, { event, log });
    setHandedOver(log);
    void sendWaitingLogs(viewer);
  });
A17
Read permissionStep (Lesson 6.1). On an Android phone, Maya answered no to the location question once, and the Hook reads { status: 'denied', granted: false, canAskAgain: true }. Which step does the function return, and what does the panel offer her? After a second no, canAskAgain is false: which step then, and what can the panel offer? (2 points)
shoreline-crew/src/hooks/usePermission.ts
export function permissionStep(response: PermissionResponse | null): PermissionStep {
  if (response === null) {
    return 'checking';
  }
  if (response.granted) {
    return 'granted';
  }
  if (response.status === 'undetermined') {
    return 'not-asked';
  }
  // The web preview's answers always say canAskAgain: true, but a browser never asks again after
  // a "Block": treat a no there as blocked
  if (!response.canAskAgain || Platform.OS === 'web') {
    return 'blocked';
  }
  return 'denied';
}

Lessons

7.1

Animation with Reanimated 4

By the end you can
  • Explain why phone animations run on the UI thread, and what shared values and worklets do about it
  • Animate with shared values: useSharedValue, useAnimatedStyle, withTiming, withSpring and withSequence, read and written with get() and set()
  • Hand a value from the UI thread back to React with useAnimatedReaction and scheduleOnRN, without drawing the screen 60 times a second
  • Use Reanimated 4's CSS-style transitions and keyframe animations, and choose between them and shared values
  • Respect the phone's Reduce Motion setting: know what Reanimated does about it by itself, and what it leaves to you
Key ideas
  • Two threads, one frame: the JavaScript thread runs React, queries and the queue; the UI thread draws and reads touches. A frame has 16.7 ms (8.3 at 120 Hz). Animating with setState per frame is only as smooth as the busiest thing on the JavaScript thread.
  • Shared values and worklets: useSharedValue, read with get() and written with set() (never .value with the React Compiler, never while rendering); useAnimatedStyle runs on the UI thread; useAnimatedReaction + scheduleOnRN hand a value back to React, as rarely as possible.
  • Timing or spring: withTiming lands exactly (numbers, bars, opacity); withSpring moves like an object and may overshoot (a bounce); withSequence, withDelay, withRepeat chain them; the finished callback runs on the UI thread.
  • CSS-style transitions and keyframes live in an Animated view's style: transitionProperty/transitionDuration for a style that changes, animationName (the keyframes object itself) for a loop.
  • Reduce Motion: Reanimated's functions jump to the end by default (ReduceMotion.System); CSS-style ones do not, so check useReducedMotion(). Both read the setting once, at start.
Worked example: the skipper and the helm, in code

The heart of Tally.tsx, from the finished code. Read it as the skipper and the helm: React sets the destination once (count.set(withTiming(value, ...))), the UI thread moves count every frame, and the reaction calls React back only when the number reaches a new whole step:

shoreline-crew/src/components/Tally.tsx
  // On the UI thread, every frame: has the count reached a new step? Then tell React (JS thread).
  useAnimatedReaction(
    () => Math.round(count.get() * steps),
    (step, previous) => {
      if (step !== previous) {
        scheduleOnRN(setShown, step / steps);
      }
    },
  );

  // Count when the screen comes into view, and when the value changes while it is in view
  useFocusEffect(
    useCallback(() => {
      if (count.get() === value) {
        return;
      }
      setCounting(true);
      const counted = {
        duration: 900,
        easing: Easing.out(Easing.cubic), // fast at first, slowing down at the end
        reduceMotion: ReduceMotion.System, // the default, written out: Reduce Motion on = jump to the end
      };
      count.set(
        withTiming(value, counted, (finished) => {
          // Runs on the UI thread when the count stops (finished is false if something interrupted it)
          if (finished) {
            scheduleOnRN(setCounting, false);
            // A bounce: grow quickly, then spring back. dampingRatio below 1 lets it overshoot a little.
            scale.set(
              withSequence(
                withTiming(1.2, { duration: 120 }),
                withSpring(1, { dampingRatio: 0.35, duration: 500 }),
              ),
            );
          }
        }),
      );
    }, [count, scale, value]),

Two shared values (count on its way, scale resting at 1), one state for the number shown. steps is 10 for kilograms (one decimal), 1 for pieces. With Reduce Motion on, withTiming jumps to the end by itself (ReduceMotion.System, written out in counted).

Guided lab
  • Open labs/module-18/l7_1-animation-with-reanimated-4/TASKS.md. A terminal in starter (nothing to install). npm test: five of eight fail.
  • Task 1: a bar that glides (a percentage that stays between 0 and 100, then a CSS-style transition). Task 2: a dot that holds still for Reduce Motion and hides from screen readers. Task 3: a counter with .value and a bouncy spring.
QuestionYour answer
Which component turns transition props into motion: View or Animated.View?

7.2

Gestures

By the end you can
  • Explain why React Native apps read gestures with React Native Gesture Handler, on the UI thread, and set it up with GestureHandlerRootView
  • Build a drag with usePanGesture and GestureDetector that moves a view through Reanimated shared values and springs it into place when the finger lets go
  • Make a sideways swipe share the screen with vertical scrolling (activeOffsetX, failOffsetY) and combine gestures on one view
  • Give every gesture a way that needs no gesture, and keep swipe rows correct in a recycled FlashList
  • Decide what a swipe may do to data that has not reached the server yet
Key ideas
  • Gesture Handler reads touches on the UI thread; its callbacks are worklets that write shared values, so a view follows the finger with no React render; scheduleOnRN tells React the result.
  • One GestureHandlerRootView (with flex: 1) around the whole app, in the root layout. Without it, a phone in development stops with a red screen; the web preview carries on.
  • Gesture Handler 3 Hooks: usePanGesture({ ... }) + <GestureDetector>; callbacks onBegin, onActivate, onUpdate, onDeactivate, onFinalize (not onStart/onEnd). The finger drives, a spring finishes.
  • Sharing the finger: activeOffsetX and failOffsetY let a swipe and a scroll share a touch; useExclusiveGestures, useCompetingGestures and useSimultaneousGestures combine gestures on one view.
  • Another way, and the data rule: every gesture has a visible button (WCAG 2.5.1); phone-only data is never removed without asking, never while sending; in a recycled list, swipe state lives in the parent, keyed by id.
Worked example: the deckhand on the line, in code

SwipeToRemove's pan, from the finished code. The config says when the gesture may start (the two offsets), what the finger does while it moves (onUpdate) and what letting go means (onDeactivate):

shoreline-crew/src/components/SwipeToRemove.tsx
  const pan = usePanGesture({
    enabled,
    activeOffsetX: [-12, 12], // 12 points sideways first: this is a swipe...
    failOffsetY: [-10, 10], // ...10 points up or down first: a scroll, so the swipe lets go
    // These run on the UI thread, many times a second, while the finger moves
    onUpdate: (event) => {
      offset.set(Math.min(0, event.translationX)); // follow the finger, leftwards only
    },
    onDeactivate: (event) => {
      if (offset.get() < -reach || event.velocityX < flick) {
        scheduleOnRN(onSwipe); // React decides what a swipe means
      }
      offset.set(withSpring(0)); // back into place, from wherever the finger let go
    },
  });

Both callbacks are worklets: they run on the UI thread, many times a second. Math.min(0, ...) lets the row go left only. Far OR fast asks; scheduleOnRN(onSwipe) tells React, which shows the question. withSpring(0) brings the row home either way (instantly with Reduce Motion on).

Lessons

7.3

Motion Polish

By the end you can
  • Animate views that appear, disappear and move with Reanimated's layout animations (entering, exiting, layout) and keep the first render still with LayoutAnimationConfig
  • Recognize where layout animations misbehave: rows recycled by FlashList, and a resizing container with moving children
  • Play a Lottie animation with lottie-react-native, set up what the web preview needs for it, and check where an animation file comes from before shipping it
  • Follow the phone's Reduce Motion setting while the app runs, with AccessibilityInfo and ReducedMotionConfig, and cover what Reanimated's setting does not reach
  • Decide whether an app needs its own motion switch, and build one that only ever adds to the phone's setting
Key ideas
  • Layout animations are three props on an Animated view: entering (mount), exiting (unmount: Reanimated keeps a copy long enough to animate it away), layout (its place changed). Keys tell them which view is which.
  • The first render is not news: <LayoutAnimationConfig skipEntering> keeps what is there when a screen opens still; later arrivals still animate.
  • Where they misbehave: layout on a box that only gets shorter fights its children's own; FlashList rows are recycled, never mounted or unmounted, so they do not animate in or out.
  • Lottie plays designer-made vector animation from a JSON file; the web preview needs @lottiefiles/dotlottie-react as well; every file has an owner and a license.
  • Reduce Motion, live: AccessibilityInfo's reduceMotionChanged feeds a store; <ReducedMotionConfig> tells Reanimated; Lottie and native screens ask useReduceMotion() themselves. The app's own switch only adds: phone || reduceMotion.
Worked example: the bench, in code

The waiting cards in the finished Logbook: one Animated.View around each SwipeToRemove, with three props. The swipe moves the card inside; the layout animation moves the whole thing:

shoreline-crew/src/app/(tabs)/logbook.tsx
      // A card that leaves slides out the way the swipe points; the cards below glide up
      <Animated.View
        key={log.clientId}
        entering={FadeIn}
        exiting={FadeOutLeft}
        layout={LinearTransition}

Read it as the bench: entering = sitting down (a log that arrives while you watch), exiting = standing up and walking off to the left (the way the swipe points), layout = the others shuffling along. Around the whole screen, <LayoutAnimationConfig skipEntering>: when the crew first sits down, nobody makes a show of it. And the rule that decides whether any of it moves:

shoreline-crew/src/state/motion.ts
export function useReduceMotion(): boolean {
  const phone = usePhoneReducesMotion();
  const { reduceMotion } = useSettings();
  return phone || reduceMotion;

Two sources, one answer, and || means the app can only ever ask for LESS motion.

8.1

Accessible Mobile Apps

By the end you can
  • Explain how VoiceOver and TalkBack move through a React Native screen, and describe an element with its role, label, state, value and (sometimes) hint
  • Audit a whole app systematically: automated checks and the accessibility tree in the web preview, then a screen reader and large text on a phone, knowing what each one can and cannot tell you
  • Hide decoration with aria-hidden, group what belongs together, and make names that start with the words people see
  • Announce changes on an iPhone, where accessibilityLiveRegion does nothing
  • Support the phone's text size: keep allowFontScaling, use maxFontSizeMultiplier only where you can justify it, and switch layouts with fontScale
  • Give every control a touch target of at least 44 by 44 points (48 by 48 in Shoreline Crew)
Key ideas
  • A screen reader meets one element at a time, in order, by ear. Each element is a radio call: label, role, state, value, sometimes a hint.
  • accessible groups a view into one element (never around controls); aria-hidden removes decoration on iPhone, Android and the web preview alike (a boolean, not the string "true").
  • An audit runs every screen and state through the web preview's checks AND a screen reader and the largest text on a phone. A clean web audit is not an accessible app.
  • Names start with the words people see (Label in Name): voice control users say them. iPhone has no live regions: announceForAccessibilityWithOptions(words, { queue: true }).
  • Large text: layouts that grow first, a fontScale layout second, maxFontSizeMultiplier only on big text, allowFontScaling={false} never. Targets: 48 points here, and the obvious thing is the target.
Worked example: one row, one target, one stop

Lesson 8.1's fix for the switch rows, the core of it. Before: the words and the switch were two stops for a screen reader, and only a 40 by 20 switch answered a finger:

shoreline-crew/src/components/SwitchRow.tsx
    <Pressable
      onPress={() => onValueChange(!value)}
      accessible={false} // not a stop of its own for screen readers: the switch is
      tabIndex={-1} // nor for a keyboard (Android, the web preview): Tab goes to the switch
      style={styles.row}
    >
      {/* Seen, not read: the switch's accessibilityLabel says the same words */}
      <AppText style={styles.label} aria-hidden>
        {label}
      </AppText>
      <Switch
        value={value}
        onValueChange={onValueChange}
        accessibilityLabel={label} // a screen reader says the words with the switch

Three moves: the row presses the switch (a tap anywhere flips it once; the switch itself takes no touches, pointerEvents: 'none'), the row is not a stop of its own (accessible={false}, tabIndex={-1}), and the words are seen, not read (aria-hidden), because the switch says them. The radio call is now one: "Reduce motion in this app, switch, off".

Lesson

8.2

Testing the App's Logic

By the end you can
  • Set up Jest with jest-expo in an Expo SDK 58 project and explain what changes from the web course's Vitest
  • Make the HQ's MSW handlers answer a phone app's requests in Node, and explain the two things that stand in the way (Expo's own fetch, and MSW 3 being an ES module)
  • Replace native modules in tests in three ways: jest-expo's automatic stand-ins, a library's own Jest mock, and your own jest.mock()
  • Test Shoreline Crew's logic where it matters most: rules (address checks, schemas), files (the CSV export), the offline queue, the log sender's five outcomes and the reminders
  • Read a failing Jest run in the terminal and tell a broken test setup from a broken app
Key ideas
  • A dry run on the dock: jest-expo makes Node look like an iPhone (Babel with Expo's preset, React Native's Jest setup, a stand-in for every Expo native module, Platform.OS 'ios'). The app's decisions are real; the world around them is pretend.
  • From Vitest: globals instead of imports, jest.fn() / jest.mock() / jest.mocked(), settings in package.json, npm test watches (npm test -- --watchAll=false runs once), never a test inside src/app/.
  • Requests: SDK 58's global fetch is Expo's; jest.environment.js keeps Node's and jest.setup.ts puts it back (twice). MSW 3 is ES modules only: the test script starts Node with --experimental-vm-modules.
  • Three stand-ins: automatic (jest-expo), the library's own mock (AsyncStorage), your own jest.mock(). An empty jest.fn() answers undefined.
  • What to test: what is invisible and costly (the queue, the sender's five outcomes, the reminders, the CSV file). Reset module state in beforeEach. Assert on ids, titles and messages, not dates.
Worked example: the sender's five outcomes

The lesson's Step 5 file, run once in a copy of the finished app. Jest lists every test of a single file:

npm test -- --watchAll=false src/state/sendLogs.test.ts
 PASS  src/state/sendLogs.test.ts
  ✓ sends a waiting log, saves it in the logbook, and takes it out of the queue
  ✓ sends nothing while the phone is offline
  ✓ marks a log the server refuses, with the server's words
  ✓ keeps the log and counts the try when the server does not answer
  ✓ keeps the log for the next sign-in when the sign-in has ended
Test Suites: 1 passed, 1 total
Tests:       5 passed, 5 total

Now the same file with the setup broken: no custom environment and no useNodeFetch lines. The app is unchanged:

 FAIL  src/state/sendLogs.test.ts
  ✕ sends a waiting log, saves it in the logbook, and takes it out of the queue
  ✓ sends nothing while the phone is offline
  ✕ marks a log the server refuses, with the server's words
  ✓ keeps the log and counts the try when the server does not answer
  ✕ keeps the log for the next sign-in when the sign-in has ended
Test Suites: 1 failed, 1 total
Tests:       3 failed, 2 passed, 5 total

Read the shape, not just the first line. The two tests that pass are the two where no answer was expected (offline, and the dropped connection); every failure shows "attempts": 1. Every request "got no answer" although MSW was listening: the requests went to Expo's fetch. Setup, not app.

C) import { describe, it, expect } from '@testing-library/react-native'
D) import * as vi from 'jest', so the HQ's vi.fn() keeps working

Lesson

8.3

Testing Screens and Flows

By the end you can
  • Write component tests with React Native Testing Library 14 and explain what changes from the web course's Testing Library: async render, userEvent.press, built-in matchers, no DOM
  • Find controls by role and name, the way a screen reader meets them, and explain why hidden elements are skipped
  • Render Expo Router screens with renderRouter, together with TanStack Query and the HQ's mock API, and handle the fake clock it switches on
  • Test hooks with renderHook and play the phone in a test: text size, platform, screen reader announcements
  • Describe an end-to-end test with Maestro, write a flow for Shoreline Crew, and say where such a flow can run
  • Decide what is worth a test, and read a coverage report without chasing its number
Key ideas
  • RNTL 14 is the web's Testing Library for native views: await render(...) (render, rerender, renderHook, fireEvent and act are async), userEvent from RNTL (press, type, clear, longPress, scrollTo), built-in matchers (toBeOnTheScreen, toBeChecked, toHaveTextContent, toHaveDisplayValue, toBeDisabled), no DOM: the tree is View, Text, TextInput, RCTSwitch.
  • Queries see what a screen reader sees: role from accessibilityRole or role, name from accessibilityLabel, aria-label or the text inside. Hidden elements (aria-hidden, screens behind a modal) are skipped unless you pass { includeHiddenElements: true }.
  • Presses follow the touch system: user.press() plays press in, press out, press, and respects pointerEvents; fireEvent(el, 'valueChange', true) sends one event by name (a screen reader flipping a native switch).
  • The pretend phone: React Native's preset window is 750 x 1334 with fontScale 2 until the setup sets a phone; Platform.OS is 'ios' (jest.replaceProperty for Android); AccessibilityInfo is a recording stand-in; Dimensions.set() inside act() plays a text-size change.
  • Screens under Expo Router: renderRouter(routes, { initialUrl }) with routes written like file names, testRouter, toHavePathname. It switches on fake timers: keep nextTick and queueMicrotask real (fakeTimers.doNotFake), and remember findBy counts fake time. Tests live in src/test/screens/, never src/app/.
  • What is worth a test: decisions a person depends on (loading, errors, guests, presses, names). Not styles, whole-tree snapshots, libraries or internals. Coverage per file is a map of what never ran, not a goal. End-to-end flows (Maestro, YAML, a device and a build) for the few paths that must work every release.
Worked example: reading the printed tree

The lesson's second Common Mistake, made for real in a copy of the finished project: a plain getByText('Group by date') to press a switch row's words. Jest prints the message, then the screen's tree (the test's eyes), then where:

● flips exactly once for a tap on the switch or on the words

  Unable to find an element with text: Group by date

  <View
    accessible={false}
  >
    <Text
      aria-hidden={true}
    >
      Group by date
    </Text>
    <RCTSwitch
      accessibilityLabel="Group by date"
      accessibilityRole="switch"
      value={true}
    />
  </View>

    37 |   expect(toggle).toBeChecked();
    38 |
  > 39 |   await user.press(screen.getByText('Group by date'));
       |                           ^

Read it in three moves. The message: no element with that text, as far as the queries can see. The tree: the words ARE there, on a Text with aria-hidden={true}, and the switch carries them as its accessibilityLabel. The cause: RNTL skips what a screen reader skips. Find the control by role and name (getByRole('switch', { name: 'Group by date' })); to press the words, as a finger can, say so: { includeHiddenElements: true }.

Lessons

8.4

Performance and Debugging

By the end you can
  • Open React Native DevTools with j, find the Expo CLI's dev tools menu, and say which tool answers which question on a phone and in the web preview
  • Explain why a component renders, measure renders with React DevTools' Profiler, and decide from numbers whether memoization is needed with the React Compiler on
  • Describe the JS thread and the UI thread, frames, release versus development builds, and the common causes of memory leaks in a React Native app
  • Measure what the app's bundle carries with npx expo export and Expo Atlas, and shrink it where the numbers say it pays
  • Debug with a method: reproduce, read the error, narrow it down, fix, and add a test that fails without the fix
Key ideas
  • The toolbox: j opens React Native DevTools for an app running Hermes (Expo Go, a development build): Console, Sources, Components, Profiler, Memory. Shift+M: the element inspector, the performance monitor, plugins (expo-sqlite, Expo Atlas). The web preview uses the browser's DevTools and the React Developer Tools extension.
  • Why a component renders: its state, its parent, a context, a subscription (TanStack Query, Zustand, useWindowDimensions). The React Compiler (on in Expo's template) remembers results for you: Shoreline Crew rendered about five times fewer components per keystroke with it. Memoize by hand only where a measurement says so.
  • Two threads, two frame rates: the JS thread (your components, hooks, handlers) and the UI thread (layout, drawing, scrolling, worklets); 16.7 ms per frame at 60 Hz. Count renders anywhere; judge speed in production mode (npx expo start --no-dev --minify, a build) on a real phone. Leaks: listeners without remove(), timers, big objects kept forever, full-size images.
  • The bundle is Hermes bytecode: 2238 modules and 5.9MB on Android before the lesson, mostly libraries. Expo Atlas shows each package's share. Shoreline Crew removed three unused packages (check with a search and npm ls) and Zod's 40 languages: 2084 modules, 5.5MB.
  • The debugging method: reproduce (a failing test is the best reproduction), read the error (the stack is a path through your imports), narrow it down (API, cache, the screen's logic), fix one thing, keep a test that fails without the fix.
  • Release stack traces point into bytecode: npx expo export --source-maps writes the map that translates them back (crash reporting, Lesson 9.3).
Worked example: the vanished cleanup, debugged with the method

The volunteer's report: "I checked in at the Tide Pool cleanup, and now it's gone from My cleanups." Step 1 of the method: reproduce it automatically, with a test. Without the jest.mock() line, the new test file does not even run:

npm test -- --watchAll=false src/test/screens/my-cleanups.test.tsx

Tests:       0 total

Step 2, read the error: the stack, read from the bottom up, is the chain of imports (the test, my-cleanups.tsx, mutations.ts, reminders.ts, expo-notifications). That is the setup, not the bug: a stand-in for @/notifications/reminders, with a factory. Run it again:

 FAIL  src/test/screens/my-cleanups.test.tsx
  ✓ lists the cleanups Theo signed up for, each with a Cancel button
  ✕ keeps today's cleanup after check-in, marked Checked in, with nothing to cancel

● keeps today's cleanup after check-in, marked Checked in, with nothing to cancel
  Unable to find an element with text: Tide Pool Morning Cleanup

Tests:       1 failed, 1 passed, 2 total

Now it fails for the volunteer's reason. Step 3, narrow it down: the API still lists the sign-up (status attended), so the bug is in what the screen keeps. Step 4, fix one thing (the filter keeps today's attended sign-up; a Checked in badge; no Cancel). Step 5, keep the test: it failed without the fix and passes with it.

 PASS  src/test/screens/my-cleanups.test.tsx

9.1

App Config and Environments

By the end you can
  • Explain what the app config controls, who reads it (Expo Go, prebuild, your build) and how to see exactly what Expo sees with npx expo config
  • Replace app.json with a typed app.config.ts and say what you gain and what tools can no longer do for you
  • Define development, preview and production variants with their own names and app ids, chosen by APP_VARIANT
  • Decide where each setting lives (EXPO_PUBLIC_ variables, the shell, .env.local, a build service's secrets) and keep secrets out of your code
  • Explain why release builds refuse plain http, what that means for shoreline-api, and what a real app does instead
  • Audit what a build asks the phone for: permission sentences, Face ID, push entitlement, notification icon and splash screen
Key ideas
  • The app config is the label on the app: name, version, icon, splash, scheme, ids, and the config plugins that write native settings. Expo Go uses only the public part; prebuild and your build use all of it. It runs in Node on your computer, never on the phone.
  • app.config.ts is a function that returns an ExpoConfig: typed (tsc catches a misspelled key that app.json would ignore), commented, able to read process.env (.env.local is loaded first). The price: npx expo install prints plugin entries for you to add instead of adding them.
  • Three variants, one setting: APP_VARIANT picks development (Shoreline Dev, .dev, the default), preview (Shoreline Preview, .preview) or production (Shoreline Crew). Different ids let all three sit on one phone; a misspelled variant stops every command with your own message.
  • Where settings live: EXPO_PUBLIC_API_URL is in the bundle (not a secret); APP_VARIANT in the shell or the build profile; the Maps key in .env.local or the build service's secrets, never in a committed file, and restricted because it ships in the app. A shell variable beats .env.local.
  • Plain http works in Expo Go and development builds; release builds refuse it (Android cleartext, iOS App Transport Security). Preview may allow it for your own Wi-Fi (expo-build-properties); production needs https, and the config refuses anything else.
  • What a build asks the phone for: permission sentences, no Face ID sentence, no push entitlement, a white notification icon, a dark splash. npx expo config --type introspect shows it today; Lesson 9.2's build shows it on a phone.
Worked example: one variable, three apps

One environment variable, three apps. With Lesson 9.1's app.config.ts, the same command gives different names and ids:

npx expo config --type public
APP_VARIANT=preview npx expo config --type public
name: 'Shoreline Dev',
usesCleartextTraffic: false
bundleIdentifier: 'com.example.shorelinecrew.dev'
package: 'com.example.shorelinecrew.dev',

name: 'Shoreline Preview',
usesCleartextTraffic: true
bundleIdentifier: 'com.example.shorelinecrew.preview'
package: 'com.example.shorelinecrew.preview',

And a mistake stops every Expo command at once, with the config's own message (the stack cut after its first line):

APP_VARIANT=prod npx expo config --type public
env: load .env.local
env: export EXPO_PUBLIC_API_URL
Error: Error reading Expo config at ~/shoreline/shoreline-crew/app.config.ts:

APP_VARIANT must be development, preview or production, not "prod".
Error: APP_VARIANT must be development, preview or production, not "prod".
    at Object.<anonymous> (~/shoreline/shoreline-crew/app.config.js:15:11)

Read the first Error: line: it is almost always enough. A misspelled KEY is different: Expo would ignore it, so npx tsc --noEmit is the check that names it (TS2561, "Did you mean to write 'bundleIdentifier'?").

Glossary for This Session

TermMeaning
React Native DevToolsThe debugger j opens for an app running Hermes: Console, Sources, Components, Profiler, Memory
performance monitorAn overlay on the phone (Shift+M) with the UI and JS frame rates
React CompilerRewrites components at build time so they reuse what they computed; on in Expo's template
JS thread / UI threadWhere your JavaScript runs / where native views are laid out, drawn and scrolled
Hermes bytecode (.hbc)The app's JavaScript compiled ahead of time: what npx expo export writes
Expo AtlasA bundle explorer: each package's share of the bundle, as a treemap
source mapThe file that translates a release build's stack trace back to your files
app configThe app's label: name, ids, icons, splash, scheme, plugins (app.json or app.config.ts)
public configThe part of the config that travels with the app (Expo Go, Constants.expoConfig)
app variant / APP_VARIANTOne of the three apps one project makes / the variable that picks it
bundle identifier / packageThe app id on iOS / on Android: a phone replaces an app with a build of the same id
cleartext traffic / App Transport SecurityPlain http, refused by Android release builds / iOS's https rule
introspectnpx expo config --type introspect: the native settings the plugins would write, in memory

Lesson

9.2

Development Builds and EAS Build

By the end you can
  • Say when Expo Go is no longer enough, and which parts of Shoreline Crew need a build of their own
  • Explain what a development build is, add expo-dev-client, and say which changes need a new build and which only need Metro
  • Write eas.json with development, preview and production profiles that set APP_VARIANT and the API address, and test them on your computer
  • Link the project to an Expo account by hand when the config is app.config.ts, and keep keys in EAS environment variables
  • Start a cloud build, read its queue and limits honestly, install the result on an Android phone or emulator, and name what an iPhone build costs
  • Choose between a cloud build and a local build, and say what each needs on your computer
Key ideas
  • Expo Go is a borrowed app with a fixed native kit, one SDK, its own name and id. A development build is your own debug app with Expo's developer tools (expo-dev-client): your native code, your label, still loading JavaScript from Metro with Fast Refresh. A release build (preview, production) has the JavaScript inside.
  • JavaScript = Metro, native = a new build. Screens, hooks, styles, tests and EXPO_PUBLIC_ values for Metro need no build; a package with native code, a config plugin option, the name, ids, icons and permission sentences do.
  • eas.json is the packing list: one build profile per kind of build (--profile); developmentClient, distribution: "internal" (an APK from a link on Android), env (this profile's variables), environment (which set of EAS environment variables), autoIncrement with appVersionSource: "remote".
  • A cloud build never sees .env.local: EAS CLI evaluates app.config.ts with the profile's env and EXPO_NO_DOTENV=1, and .gitignore keeps the file out of the upload. Addresses go in the profile; keys go in EAS environment variables (sensitive).
  • With app.config.ts, EAS cannot write the project id: paste owner and extra.eas.projectId into easProject yourself.
  • Costs, honestly: a free account and the free plan build Android and iOS within a monthly limit, in a low-priority queue (check Expo's pricing page); a real iPhone needs the paid Apple Developer Program; Android builds install free. Local builds (npx expo run:android) trade setup for queue time.
Worked example: a profile tested before a build

The lesson's profile test reads each profile's env and runs app.config.ts with it, the way EAS CLI does (no .env.local). All three pass on the finished code:

npm test -- --watchAll=false app.config
 PASS  ./app.config.test.ts
  ✓ builds Shoreline Dev from the development profile
  ✓ builds Shoreline Preview from the preview profile, with an API address
  ✓ refuses the production profile until it has an https API address
Tests:       3 passed, 3 total

One misspelled word in eas.json, "APP_VARIANT": "prevue", passes EAS's own eas.json check (it cannot know your variants) and fails the test with Lesson 9.1's message:

● builds Shoreline Preview from the preview profile, with an API address
  APP_VARIANT must be development, preview or production, not "prevue".

Read it from the top: which test (the preview profile), the message (the guard you wrote), then the code frame (line 21 of app.config.ts). Caught in a fraction of a second on your computer instead of minutes into a cloud build that counts against your month.

Glossary

TermMeaning
development buildA debug build of your own app with Expo's developer tools; loads JavaScript from Metro
expo-dev-clientThe package that turns a debug build into a development build (launcher, developer menu)
release buildA build with the JavaScript inside (preview, production): no Metro, no developer menu
EAS BuildExpo's cloud build service (Expo Application Services); driven by npx eas-cli@latest
build profileOne entry in eas.json's build section, picked with --profile
internal distributionInstalled directly from a link, not from a store (an APK on Android; ad hoc on iPhones)
APK / AABThe Android file a phone installs from a link / the App Bundle Google Play wants
EAS environment variablesValues stored on expo.dev per environment (plain text, sensitive, secret)
keystoreThe key that signs an Android app; EAS can create and keep it
ad hoc provisioningApple's way to install a build on registered iPhones (paid membership)
build queueThe line a cloud build waits in for a free server (low priority on the free plan)
local buildnpx expo run:android / run:ios / eas build --local: built on your own computer

Lessons

9.3

Updates and Monitoring

By the end you can
  • Explain how expo-updates and EAS Update deliver new JavaScript and assets to an installed build, and when a phone starts using them
  • Choose a runtime version policy, and measure on your computer whether a change needs a new build or can go out as an update
  • Connect build profiles to update channels, and publish an update to the right builds with the right environment variables
  • Say what an over-the-air update may and may not change, under Expo's technical limits and the app stores' rules
  • Tell the user-facing version from build numbers, and add a crash screen and a reporting seam that a crash-reporting service can plug into
  • Write a GitHub Actions workflow that runs the type check, lint and tests on every push, including the step a fresh checkout of an Expo project needs
Key ideas
  • Two parts of every app: the native part (compiled into the build) and the JavaScript part with its assets. expo-updates replaces the second part in a release build: it asks at every start, downloads in the background, runs the update at the NEXT start, and falls back to the embedded code.
  • Runtime version: a label for the native part; a phone takes only updates with exactly its own. Shoreline Crew uses the fingerprint policy: npx expo-updates runtimeversion:resolve measures it before you publish.
  • Channels come from build profiles (preview, production). npx eas-cli@latest update --channel preview --environment preview --message "…" publishes; the variant's settings must be in the EAS environment, because eas update does not read the profile's env.
  • What an update may change: JavaScript and assets (fixes, text, styles); never native code, and never what the app is (store rules). Rollback, republish and percentage rollouts are updates too.
  • Version vs build number: the version (1.0.0) is yours and user-facing; build numbers live on EAS and count up. An update changes neither: Profile gets an Updates line.
  • Monitoring and CI: a root ErrorBoundary shows CrashScreen; every error goes through reportError, and setReporter can plug a service in later. CI runs npm ci, npx expo customize tsconfig.json, tsc, lint and the tests on every push.
Worked example: build or update? measure it

Does a change need a new build? Measure it. The runtime version is a fingerprint of everything native; a phone takes only updates with exactly its own. On the course app (only the runtimeVersion of each line kept):

npx expo-updates runtimeversion:resolve --platform android
APP_VARIANT=preview npx expo-updates runtimeversion:resolve --platform android
29268b22f86138bf4fc840f53d9fee4a7e94525a
142af542088aaca299daa3c4fdc8d957b9068665

Two variants, two runtime versions. A comment in the Logbook screen: the preview hash stays 142af542… (JavaScript: an update). A reworded location permission sentence: a510c337… (native: a build). The rule of thumb: what ends up in the JavaScript bundle (screens, text, styles, EXPO_PUBLIC_ values) can travel by update; what is compiled into the native project (packages with native code, permission sentences, the icon, the name, the ids) needs a build. The measurement decides.

  • Open labs/module-24/l9_3-updates-and-monitoring/TASKS.md. A terminal in starter: npm test (one passes, eight fail).
  • Task 1: takesUpdate in reach.ts. Task 2: scrub and scrubbing in scrub.ts. Task 3 (fast finishers): .github/workflows/ci.yml.
